To F. N.

Murder Is Not Mute

Detective Patrick Michael Doyle was enjoying, to quote his wife, a lovely vacation at the seashore. A lovely vacation at the seashore, to quote Detective Patrick Michael Doyle, was about as enjoyable as a pain in the neck. 

Ellen Doyle said, "Go on, she could see right through him." Which remarkable power of vision showed her that it was the nasty, stubborn streak in him which wouldn't admit how wonderful things were at the 

Seaview Hotel. 

To her husband's scornful inquiry as to what comprised these wonders, Ellen spoke loftily of the refined atmosphere. Ellen was a great one for refinement, as she frequently asserted. And it certainly wouldn't hurt 

Doyle to rub shoulders with it, all the time mixing up with cops and criminals and lawyers and such. 

So the first thing Ellen did after the bags were unpacked was to make friends with some lovely, refined ladies. Their husbands were refined, too. 

For one dreary week Doyle bore with this grim group, and then he fled, sneaking off for a solitary

walk-though both the exercise and the road he followed, stretching steel-bright under the August sun, beguiled him only because they led resolutely away from the Seaview Hotel.

Certainly the road had no charms other than its direction. Detective Doyle shared it with a steady procession of automobiles whose horns berated him for the small area he occupied on its melting tar surface. It skirted, at intervals, the architectural horrors of Filling Stations and Hot Dog Stands. And it flaunted a garish art gallery which depicted ruddy gentlemen and streamlined ladies ardently regarding cigarettes, soap, beverages, motorcars, or their own shapely, silk-garbed legs. 

The thing to do," Doyle decided, " is to find myself some trees. And sit under 'em." 66 Shortly thereafter, casting a speculative eye over the landscape, he discerned the faint trail of a path which struck out across the scorched fields toward a not too distant woodland. He took to this path with alacrity, and thus, unwitting, made the first move toward the strange adventure it held in store for him.

The trail ended blankly in a stone wall, behind which barrier a dense growth of trees and underbrush cast beguiling gloom. But there were signs posted. Trespassers would be prosecuted. Doyle stood for a moment regarding the sign nearest him. "Aw, the hell with it," he decided. And heaved his considerable bulk up and over the wall. 

As he felt the welcome shade close in on him, he took off the incongruous derby which he wore regardless of season, and complacently mopped his brow. The path, in defiance of the wall, cut its way on through the forest, inviting Doyle to its cooler depths. 10

He trailed along thinking, why was it every year there had to be this damned nonsense of three weeks vacation? Why couldn't Ellen refine herself to her heart's content on beach or mountain and let her husband stay comfortably in Manhattan where he could keep in touch with his colleagues on the Homicide 

Squad? Because the truth was, take him away from his work and he was empty and useless to himself and everyone else.

Doyle found, as he forced his way through the undergrowth, that the path was increasingly impassable. "Too damn many bushes," he complained. But the trees he considered O.K. Tall and straight they stood, like so many Empire State Buildings. Looking up at them, Doyle felt a breeze ruffle his wavy crop of silvering black hair a breeze which came with sudden gusto and set the leaves dancing, then a moment later died away as suddenly as it had risen. 

Now Doyle was dimly aware of a waiting quality in the ensuing stillness. Nothing stirred. The murmurous voice of the forest, the songs of the birds, were strangely hushed. But unskilled in meteorological observation, he pressed on, not recognizing the tense suspension of sound and movement which precedes a forest storm. 

For a while he lost the path, but found it again, turning sharply right. Then a few steps farther on, his progress was blocked by a boulder. Doyle regarded the big rock curiously. It had a lot of initials and hearts and dates cut into it, and up near the top someone had chipped out the words TABLE ROCK. 

Evidently people used to come here to picnic. Until some Stuffed Shirt got hold of the property and put up the wall and the No Trespassing signs. On the other 11

hand you had to consider that picnickers could make messy nuisances of themselves and a man has a right to protect his property. 

Where did this guy live, anyway? In the trees?

Doyle climbed up on the boulder and took a look around. On the other side of the rock he could discern the outlines of the path resuming its interrupted course. 

But the heat and the stillness filled Doyle with lethargy. He sat down on the sun-baked stone. His eyes, intensely blue in his square-jawed, weather-beaten face, stared idly at the big black clouds massing overhead. 

Then as his gaze fell, he detected, close to his feet, a curious coiled pattern among the wavering shadows. 

Instantly the detective's apathy was dispelled. He arose with scrambling haste, rousing from its torpor the snake which had sunned itself all afternoon on Table Rock, and which now prepared for immediate departure with a swift uncoiling that was near to being the death of Doyle. He jerked back, lost his balance, and plunged, arms wildly waving, off the rock and down into a thicket of underbrush. "God save us!" he ejaculated when he had regained his feet. 

He took swift inventory. No bones broken, praise be.

But his face and hands were badly scratched. He fished out his handkerchief and stanched a cut over his right eye, then ministered with tender concern to his derby, dangling disconsolately from an overhead branch. 

It was at this moment that, with deplorably theatric effect, an ominous rumble of thunder smote the woods. 

Involuntarily Doyle turned. Then his gaze riveted, his muscles tensed, and a shiver crawled through his body. 12

Half-hidden by the undergrowth, close to where he stood, there lay a human skeleton. 

The detective fumbled for his cigarettes. He needed the familiar ritual of a smoke to ease himself over the succession of startling moments, and to enable him to view the scene about him with some approximation of professional detachment. Above and around him the forest sighed and twisted in the rising gale, and screened from view both the path and the boulder. 

Doyle broke his way over to the skeleton and, peering down at it, sniffed experimentally. This bony structure bore little resemblance to the immaculate, neat arrangements he'd seen illustrated in books or displayed in doctors' offices. It was with considerable squeamish reluctance that he pushed aside the intervening branches for a closer view. 

The skeleton lay face down, the skull turned sharply sideways, and the arm bones fully extended at right angles from the torso. The position of the bones indicated that there had been neither peace nor repose when the last fainting breath had left the body. Here in the lonely forest, Death had struck violently. 

Suddenly the look of the man hunter made Doyle's eyes steely and the set of his square jaw formidable. He went down on his knees, now thoroughly oblivious to the discomforts of closer acquaintance with the unsavory bones, as he searched for evidence to confirm his quick, sinister suspicion.

Almost immediately he found what he sought. This was neither natural death nor suicide. This was murder. 

A bullet had gone neatly through the victim's skull, at an angle which proved suicide impossible, and al- 13

though decomposition of skin, flesh, and organs had taken place, the path of the bullet was still traceable. 

For the toughest membrane of the body, the scalp, was not yet completely decayed. It showed, plainly, the aperture which the entrance of the bullet had caused at the back of the head, and the aperture where it had crashed its way out through the frontal bone. There were unmistakable evidences of pressure from without around the wound of entrance, and of pressure from within around the wound of exit. All of which, plus the prone position of the skeleton, indicated that the murderer had fired when the victim's back was turned. 

Were these the bones of man or woman?

Doyle dismissed the question with a rueful shrug of his heavy shoulders, and looked about him, uncomfortably astonished to see how dark the woods had become. 

It occurred to him belatedly that a storm must be brewing.

The sensible thing to do would be to get the hell out of here and back to the Seaview before the storm broke. 

But the wisdom of this course had no chance against the appeal of its alternative, which was to continue on from the boulder and seek shelter at the end of the path. 

Doyle was curious to learn where the path went, and who owned the property through which it passed, though he was well aware that it was his immediate duty to report his discovery of the skeleton to the local police. But at the thought his jaw set stubbornly. The bones had been here a long time, hadn't they? A little further delay would do no harm. He'd just have the fun of snooping around a bit before he went to the police. 

Accordingly he broke through the undergrowth to 14

the far side of the boulder and plodded along the path. 

He kept with difficulty to its tortuous windings and saw nothing but woodland such as he had already traversed, yet he was unaccountably buoyed by a sense of impending adventure. "The guy who built that wall must live somewhere inside it," he reasoned doggedly.

To his disgust, the storm broke uproariously, and almost immediately the slanting lines of rain changed to a barrage of hailstones. Doyle began to run, turning up his coat collar, as they bounced merrily from the hard contours of his derby.

At last, to his gratification, he emerged from the woodland into open country. Surely now he'd see a house. His inquiring gaze swept the rolling terrain hopefully. Straight ahead rose a mound of earth, its artificially sloping sides covered with coarse grass, its crest levelled off with closely mown clover. Atop the mound and somewhat off-center, a flag twisted dankly in the wind. On the flag was the number 13. 

The blood of Doyle's superstitious forbears surged uneasily. "Sure it's only the thirteenth green of a golf course," he reassured himself, and his discomfiture was further alleviated when he discovered, some hundred yards to his left where three fairways ran parallel, a small shed, erected to protect golfers from just such weather as this.

Doyle ran for it, holding his head well down to protect his eyes from the stinging hail. And so it was that he entered the shed blindly, and did not know until the moment of collision that it was already occupied. 15

ruman Briault lounged in the doorway of the shelter, resentfully watching the storm and waiting for the chauffeur to come to his rescue. 

Suddenly the young man's handsome grey eyes widened in astonishment. A stranger was running toward him from the thirteenth green. The next moment the runner reached the shed and barged blindly into Briault whose long, lean body recoiled distastefully. "Where the hell did you come from?" he demanded irritably. 

The man's face jerked up with a startled expression, and it took a second for him to get his breath, while 

Briault's arrogant stare made note of mud-spattered clothes and the unprepossessing gash over one eye. "I'm asking how the devil you got here?" The young man repeated his question with a slight uneasiness of tone, and the newcomer took a long, sharp look before he made up his mind to answer. "Keep your shirt on, Buddy. I walked here." "Then walk yourself out of here," Briault ordered. "We turn tramps over to the police." 16

Doyle blinked. Then he spoke unctuously. "O.K. with me, Buddy. I'm looking for the police." "I don't want any of your impudence," Briault said. "You're on private property and you have no business here. Now get out." 

Doyle's grin was imperturbable. "Maybe I'm not all that has no business here. Who owns this place anyway?" of 66 

Briault was staring at Doyle oddly. "That's none your affair," he said mechanically.

Doyle let it go at that. But then he wanted to know, Anybody ever disappear around here? "

For a moment something like panic stared starkly from the young man's eyes, before he challenged scornfully," Are you crazy? " "It might be I have the D.T.'s," Doyle confessed genially. "A little while back I saw a snake. And right after that I saw a skeleton."

The young man laughed shortly. " A skeleton in our woods? Nonsense." "I didn't say anything about your woods." 

Disconcerted by this reminder, Briault busied himself with a cigarette. It was while his face was masked by his cupped hands that he inquired, "What kind of a skeleton?" 

Doyle waited deliberately until the white, longfingered hands came down. Then he looked at the young man squarely. "The kind you're thinking it is." 66 "A woman? "

The moment he'd said it, Briault's jaw clamped as if he would bite back the words. 

66 I guess you know more about it than I do," Doyle spoke softly. "All the things I notice about women 17

were missing." He grinned again, his eyes fixed attentively on his companion.

Briault was drawing nervously on his cigarette. 

His forehead was deeply creased, as though he pondered what to do. And then, through the pelt of the rain, came the sound of an automobile advancing somewhere behind the shed. Briault went into instant action. 

He motioned Doyle back into the shadows of the far corner and ordered tersely, "Keep your mouth shut."

Then he posted himself in the entrance, blocking it. 

The motor was purring now, throttled low; and a car door slammed. Then a uniformed chauffeur rounded the shed. "Hello, Follins," Briault said nervously.

The chauffeur was carrying a raincoat and rubbers. 

He had a big umbrella, such as doormen use, over his head.

Briault took the coat and overshoes. “Just a second, I'll help you," the chauffeur said, none too graciously. He wrestled with the umbrella, trying to release its catch and move out of the wind into the shed. But Briault prevented that. "You needn't bother," he said quickly. “I'm not coming with you." 

The chauffeur gaped. "But your caddy said— 99 "I've changed my mind," Briault interrupted irritably. "I want to finish my game." The chauffeur looked out at the storm involuntarily. "This is just a shower," Briault explained, as the thunder cracked down out of the sky like the blast of doomsday. "Then do you want me to send your caddy back?” 

Follins queried blankly. 18

Briault shook his head quickly. "No, no. I'll finish the round alone."

It obviously didn't make sense to Follins. And just then the thing happened that Briault obviously was trying to prevent. Follins' gaze slid past Briault and focused on Doyle, standing back in the corner. A startled, conjecturing look came into the chauffeur's eyes. "You may go, Follins," Briault said crisply.

When the sound of the car had died away in distance, Briault put on the raincoat and rubbers. "Come along," he said peremptorily. "Show me that skeleton." 

Doyle shook his head. "Like hell I will. Wait until it stops raining." 

Briault began to shout angrily, protesting the delay.

Then, abruptly, he stemmed his tirade, seeming to recognize its uselessness. He smoked one cigarette after another, moodily, his gestures jerky, his facial muscles tense. And his breath was released in a gusty sigh of relief when, at last, his companion announced, “It's letting up."

Even as Doyle spoke, the arc of a rainbow spanned the sky, and for a moment the two men stared up at it, forgetful of each other and of their errand-full of the queer hushed feeling that comes with looking at a rainbow. The birds began to call tentatively. And suddenly the glowing sun was framed with azure, and raindrops shimmered and sparkled on leaves and grass. "O.K.," Doyle said briskly. "Let's go."

They picked up the path and plunged into the woods, making their difficult way in silence. When they came 19

to the boulder, Doyle led sharp right and thrust into the bushes. “Here we are,” he said unnecessarily.

Briault stared down at the skeleton and made no comment. His furtive sidelong glance showed he was aware that Doyle was watching him closely, and his face tightened in an effort to maintain inscrutability. He looked sick and defenceless. "Who is it?" Doyle asked leadingly. 

But the young man shook his head. "I haven't any idea." "What do you think we ought to do about it?" "What is there to do now?" Briault answered absently. He was staring again at the horrible sight as if morbidly fascinated by it.

Doyle suggested drily," We could notify the police." 

The young man's shoulders twitched. "Yes, of course." His tone was a sorry attempt at naturalness. 

And suddenly he cried, " For God's sake, let's get out of here.”

But once back on the path, he halted, mopped at his brow, seemed to come to swift decision. 

He spoke abruptly. "Where are you bound for?" 

Doyle told him laconically, "Beachville." "And then where?" "What's wrong with Beachville? ” 

Briault answered impatiently. "Don't let's beat around the bush. Why should you hang around Beachville? One place is as good as another to your sort, isn't it?" 

Doyle looked at him oddly, without response.

Briault continued more persuasively, "Get on out of town. Like a good fellow. We can't have a lot of ugly 20

publicity about this thing. You can understand that, can't you? 66 99 

Sure I understand," Doyle said. “You want me to keep my mouth shut.” "That's it," Briault agreed. "We don't want reporters and photographers and God knows who else overrunning the place. It would spoil our summer." "I guess that's so." Doyle eyed him shrewdly. " But the guy who lies in those bushes isn't having much of a summer. There's his side to it, too," he suggested. 

The young man spoke hastily. "You're right. That will have to be investigated." 66 

By whom?" "The police, of course." 

"You mean you'll tell them?" The question was frankly sceptical. "Certainly." "Then what's the idea of wanting me to keep quiet? 99 "Use your head," Briault retorted angrily. “If you'll keep your mouth shut, it's probable we can arrange with the local authorities to conduct the investigation secretly. What do you care how the matter is handled? What do you care about anything, as long as you can get plenty of whiskey? Isn't that so?"

Doyle's face twisted strangely, but his tone was weighted with awe. "You know a lot about human nature, don't you, mister?" he said. "I know your kind,” Briault admitted complacently. 

His brow was no longer knotted and troubled. His expression was almost gay. He put his hand into his pocket, drew out his wallet and removed the bills it contained. He held them toward Doyle. 21

"What's that for?" the detective asked guilelessly. “There's a lot of money there," Briault told him. "And there's some advice that goes with it. Don't come back for more. If you do, you'll get yourself into trouble. Serious trouble." "Put your money away, Buddy," Doyle said shortly. 

Briault stared at him. "Put it away, you fool," Doyle said more harshly.

Searching the detective's hard, knowing face, meeting his enigmatical stare, Briault blurted in bewilderment, "What is this? Who are you?" "It doesn't matter," Doyle said, "who I am." He pointed into the thicket. "Who is that? "

But Briault did not answer. Routed and dismayed, he wheeled and fled down the path, stumbling and lurching in his anxiety to be gone. 22

F3a 

Doyle went around the boulder and waited. There was a lot of money in that roll, he reflected. Maybe it would pay better to give up detecting and turn hobo. 

He grinned. If Ellen knew he'd been mistaken for a tramp she'd carry out her constant threat to part him and his ancient derby.

When he was convinced the young man was not coming back, Doyle paid the skeleton another visit to see if he could find a bullet embedded in the tree nearest the skull. He examined the bark carefully but without success. It might be that after a certain length of time the bark would grow over a foreign body. Or the bullet might have struck in another direction-the victim pinwheeling about after being shot.

What most puzzled Doyle was why the body had been left so close to the path. This hiding place had been, apparently, effective, but why would a murderer deliberately chance its proximity to a trail which had certainly been at one time much travelled? Could it be that the murderer and his victim had come through the woods toward the path without knowing or suspecting 23

its existence? What would Doyle find if he struck off into the woods at right angles to the path? 

But it was too late for exploration. The sun was sending long arrows of gold through the trees, and soon it would be dusk in the woods. Still the detective lingered, held by his conjectures.

How long ago since two people had come to this lonely spot, one of them to remain forever? How long would it take, in this climate, for clothing to rot and flesh to decompose? Who was the handsome young man who had tried to bribe Doyle? Was he the murderer? "Not likely," the detective decided. "That guy wouldn't have the guts for murder.”

Still, you never could tell. After all, the victim had been shot from behind. And it was significant that the young man had leaped to the immediate conclusion that the victim was a woman. Certainly he had the appearance that invites woman trouble. There was a faint foreign flavor to his mannerisms and intonation. His dress and the cast of his features marked him right out of the top drawer. He'd go over big with women. But not so big with men. Men would get wise right off to something shifty and untried in his character. 

Doyle consulted his bulky pocket watch. Hell. Ellen would be having fits.

With a last wistful look at the bones, he turned away. And instantly wheeled about again. His trained, observant eyes had caught sight of something, but scarcely registered it in that moment of preoccupation. 

Where? What? He frowned and repeated the movement he had made-but this time slowly, alert for impression. There it was. A faint glitter close to what had been the left hand. 24

He stepped around the bones, searching the leaves. 

Probably a shred of cellophane from his cigarette pack. 

He'd smoked when he was here the first time. Then he saw it again and swooped upon it, silver-white against the silver-white underside of a dried leaf.

It was a curious little gadget, rectangular in shape, about one-half by three-quarters of an inch in size, of the thickness of heavy paper, and made of some white metal, with a border of delicate engraving. Doyle's big hands, with their surprisingly sensitive conical fingers, discovered that the gadget separated into two partsan undecorated prong-like affair which clamped on to the engraved piece.

He wrapped it carefully in tinfoil from his cigarette pack and stowed it in his wallet. "But damned if I know what it is," he told himself ruefully. 

During the thunderstorm Officer Madden had found traffic conditions particularly trying on the five highways which converged in the center of the town of 

Beachville. When the storm was over, he retired to the peace and safety of the Bank steps to catch his breath.

But before this could be accomplished to his satisfaction, a stranger rounded the corner and drew up at the officer's elbow. Madden viewed the unknown one with an asperity which was not dispelled by the man's first words. "I want to report a murder."

The officer's Adam's apple rode up and down on a gulp of astonishment. He said nothing for the simple reason that he could think of nothing to say. "Is there a police court here?" 25

At that Madden rallied sufficiently to inquire in hushed tones," Who did you kill?” "Get this straight," Doyle urged. "I didn't kill anyone. But I found a skeleton. This afternoon. In the woods. There's been a murder, and the proper authorities should be notified. I'm asking you where to find them."

Madden's evident distaste for the stranger increased. 

Skeletons? Murder? In Beachville? His expression indicated plainly that this guy was some kind of a nut. "If there is any such thing in the Village limits," the officer said sceptically, "I'd be the one to look into it, if it's in the Village limits." “I wouldn't know the answer to that one," Doyle retorted impatiently. "You'd better come with me and take a look." "How far is it?" "Straight out the road that runs past the Seaview." "Yah!" the officer said derisively. "There aren't any woods on that road." "There are if you strike off across the fields."

Madden pondered. Then he whistled one long-drawn note, and looked at Doyle with startled attention. But all he said was, "That's private property. You have no business 99 99 "O.K.," Doyle interrupted. " I was trespassing.'

Madden surveyed him darkly." We'd better talk this over with Judge Blain," the officer decided.

Judge Blain lived in a medium-sized frame house, set close to the street and weathered a dingy mustard color. Over the driveway was a wooden arch on which was largely lettered 26

BLAIN'S BLAMELESS BUILDING BLOCKS 

The Judge was in the back yard. He was wearing denim overalls. Blameless blocks were piled up all around him, and there was a concrete mixer chugging away in the barn. 

Madden led the Judge out of earshot. Doyle could see the Judge inclining his long, narrow head to Madden's report. The Judge's mouth made a tight thin line beneath his long nose. 

He came over to Doyle, mopping at his grey hair curled damply on his perspiring brow. "I'm a busy man,” he said tersely." This isn't a joke? A wild goose chase?" "There's been a murder," Doyle said patiently. "There's a skeleton in the woods with a bullet hole through its skull.”

Blain peered at him from eyes which were darkly penetrating, deep-set under bushy black brows. "The skeleton of a deer-a murdered deer, maybe?" 

Officer Madden's round red countenance appeared over

Blain's shoulder. The officer was beginning to enjoy the situation. 

Doyle said in level tones, "A human skeleton." 

Judge Blain spoke abruptly. "You come back here in the morning. No point traipsin' through the woods in the dark." He nodded curtly and returned to the barn, followed by the officer.

There being nothing else to do, Doyle returned to the Seaview where he found himself late for dinner, and markedly unpopular with both Ellen and the Management. 27

Early the next morning Doyle was sitting on some of the Blameless Blocks when the Judge emerged from his back door. The Judge looked at Doyle and grunted.

Then he went into the barn where the mixer was churning the makings for another batch. Blain turned the machine off, poured the mix into molds, and went back into the house to wash his hands. When he came out on the porch, his wife followed him—a flustered, loudly querulous woman. Doyle thought, sympathetically, maybe she got the way she was from having to talk louder than the mixer. "You goin' to be back for lunch?” she shrieked at her husband. 

Blain said," Sure."

Doyle could tell from the way the Judge said it that he thought he was going on a fool's errand. 

Blain's shabby car was in the yard. He yanked open a rear door and addressed Doyle for the first time. "Get in."

They picked up Madden outside the Bank. The officer sat up front with the Judge, and they rattled off on the short drive, following Doyle's directions. Just beyond Krimp's Hot Dogs, the Judge drove the car bumpily across the fields, and left it parked beside the wall. When they reached the boulder, Doyle turned sharp left and wedged through the underbrush, followed by Blain and Madden.

The soft gloom of forest light bathed the thicket and an eerie hush brooded over it. But the wild beauty of the scene was lost upon the three who stood there. 

For Doyle stared at the ground and his companions stared at him. Then their eyes shifted simultaneously to meet in a knowing glance. Madden's hand lifted sur- 28

reptitiously to point to his temple, and his forefinger described a series of circles. Blain's lips folded inward and his head nodded agreement. Again they looked at 

Doyle who still stared at the ground.

There was no sign of the skeleton that had sprawled, gruesome and mysterious, in this hidden spot. 

Doyle turned, glowering. "It was right here," he told them. "It must have been- 99 He broke off abruptly, aware of the oddly lenient look with which the two men were regarding him. "Aw, cut it out," he protested. “Here. Take a look at that if you think I don't know what I'm talking about." He produced his badge and gave a brief account of himself. 

Judge Blain held out his hand. "Sorry I mistrusted you, Mr. Doyle. But you know how it is." 

Doyle said he did. 99 

Officer Madden explained heartily, "We don't put much trust in you summer people. You think because which way. you come from the City you can act every 

He did not offer to shake hands. His dubious look said clearly that for all the badge and the big talk, where was this here skeleton?

So Doyle lost no time in blocking out its former position; and though Madden continued to hold back, the 

Judge made a careful examination of the spot and its environs. In a few minutes he had disclosed the faint depressions in the wet leaf mold which had been caused by the pressure of the bones and, at the time of their removal, carefully covered over with dead leaves. 

Broken branches here and there in the shrubbery indicated that the skeleton had been carried off in some 29

sort of bag slung over the right shoulder of the carrier, who had been at pains to smooth over the traces of his footprints. "Looks to me like the footprints were tamped down with the back of a shovel," the Judge concluded. 

Madden's interest was now thoroughly aroused. "That's a dirty trick," he mourned. "How we goin' to tell where the skeleton went with all the marks rubbed out?" 

Blain said comfortingly, "Can't any man go through woods and cover all the signs. I'll trace him." "You're damned good," Doyle said warmly. "Where did you get your training?" "Deer hunting. Every Fall," Blain explained in his laconic fashion. "What would a guy do with a skeleton?" Doyle pondered. "Where would he take it?" 66 

Judge Blain jerked a thumb back over his shoulder. 

Only half a mile or so to the Bay," he said. 

Doyle scowled. "That would be a hell of a thing." 

Madden gaped from one to the other. "What you guys talkin' about, anyway?" 

At that Doyle's face brightened. "The bones aren't in the Bay," he said. "There wasn't time enough for that. That's why he brought the shovel." "I don't follow you," Blain objected. "We know the skeleton wasn't moved last night," 

Doyle pointed out, "because the tracks couldn't have been covered in the dark. Whoever took it waited for daylight. But he can't just stand on shore and pitch the bones into the Bay. He's got to go out in a boat to get depth. And he can't do that in daylight—not with people swimming and fishing all about to see him do it. 30

So he's waiting for tonight. He's buried the skeleton temporarily. It's up to us to get it before he does." "We'll get it," Blain promised. He turned to Madden. "Drive back to my house. Bring a couple of shovels. Likely we'll do some digging." "How'll I know where to find you?" Madden spluttered. 

The Judge considered briefly. " Meet you at Table 

Rock half an hour from now."

As Madden crashed his way toward the path, Doyle called to him, “Might as well bring the coroner back with you."

Blain was as good as his word and had little difficulty picking up the trail. He pointed out to Doyle the wedge-shaped depressions in the wet leaves which here and there betrayed it. "Damned if those look like human footprints,' 

Doyle objected. 99 "Just toe prints," Blain explained. "Running, he was."

They found that the trail bore steadily north and in a fairly straight line. Whoever had preceded them had been familiar with the lay of the land and had made without indecision for a clearly defined goal. When the 

Judge remarked upon this, it turned Doyle's thoughts to the young man who had tried to bribe him. 66 99 

He inquired abruptly, "Who owns this property?

A man's garments would withstand exposure longer, and we'd find buttons and metallic parts. Now if this woman was killed last summer, she probably wore a dress and an undergarment of silk or cotton; thin stockings, or none at all; low-cut shoes, more than likely of cloth; and probably a hat of straw or fabric-all of which would disintegrate and leave little or no trace. Blake and Madden were headed right to find Doyle, waiting for them in the pine grove. Then the Judge set off on an investigation of his own, following the path from Table Rock on toward Wunsch's golf course. 

This skeleton business was upsetting, and the quicker it was disposed of, the better. It would give Beachville a bad name. Make respectable folks afraid to summer there.

So the Judge wasn't wasting time watching a skeleton dug up. He'd take Dr. Blake's word that there was a skeleton, and that there'd been a murder. The common sense thing to do was to get the man who removed the skeleton. Because that man knew the answers. Like as not, he knew why the skeleton was lying in the woods. 

For certain sure, he knew why he didn't want it found. 

Judge Blain meant to get hold of that fellow and make him tell what he knew. Then dispense justice as quickly and quietly as possible. 

Too bad this city detective, Doyle, had stuck his nose into things. Not that he'd get anywhere. Because he 41

was blind as a newborn kitten. Twice, as they followed the trail through the woods, Blain had removed shreds of bluish yarn from sharp-pronged twigs. But the bateyed detective hadn't seen either the yarn or its removal. 

Too busy asking fool questions.

The Judge took from his pocket the two bits of twig tipped with fuzzy wool. Sweater yarn, sure as taxes. 

Now all Blain had to do was find someone on Wunsch's estate wearing a bluish sweater. That would be the fellow who knew the answers. 

The Judge was putting the twigs back into his pocket when all of a sudden it came to him, who'd wear a sweater a hot day like this? But after a bit of pondering he cleared that up. Daybreak was about a quarter to five, these mornings, and it would be chilly and damp in the woods then. Which fixed the time when the skeleton was moved and buried. 

It was as the Judge emerged from the forest opposite the thirteenth green that his penetrating huntsman's glance caught sight of another shred of bluish yarn on a twig almost touching his left elbow. "What do you know about that?" the Judge asked himself as close to jubilation as was possible for his dour nature. 

Whoever had buried the skeleton must have fled by this same path. And come to think of it, of course he would. He'd come through the woods to the golf course for the same reason the Judge had. Because that was the shortest route cross-country to the Wunsch house. 

Blain removed the third telltale bit of yarn. Elbowhigh on the left side, it was. The other specimens had been shoulder-high on the right side. He figured that was because the skeleton had been carried over the right 42

shoulder, so the right sleeve projected and caught on things. But what made the left sleeve catch here, after the skeleton had been disposed of? Maybe it was still the right sleeve. Maybe the fellow had turned off the path into the shrubbery.

Blain followed suit, forcing his way into the undergrowth. It didn't surprise him much to see the shovel, flung hard onto the ground so that its scoop had dug into the soft loam and branches closed down over the handle, almost concealing it. Doyle had figured right about one thing. The fellow meant to come back tonight, fetch the shovel from this hiding place, and dig up the skeleton.

The Judge pondered briefly and decided to let the shovel lie. There'd be no point in the fellow coming for it once the Judge tracked him down. Which ought to be any minute now. 

With grim purpose Blain stepped out on the golf course and set off for Wunsch's house. Because of the rolling terrain he could not see far in any direction. 

And so it was that he heard a man shout close at hand, though there was no one visible. The Judge hastily veered from his course and sprinted up the steep incline of a trap, and there, about a hundred yards away, a fellow was standing with his back turned. He was dressed in grey slacks and a blue shirt; and he was shouting to a heavy-set man some two hundred yards beyond. The wind was blowing Blain's way and he could catch most of what was said.

The heavy-set man was rubbing his shoulder and swearing lustily. "Do you want to kill me?" he roared. 

The tall fellow called out sullenly, "Sorry. That slice of mine fools me." 43

The heavy-set man kicked at the grass and a ball rolled, flashing white in the sun. Apparently he'd been hit by the other fellow's ball.

Wunsch's face underwent a curious transformation. 

It seemed to collapse, as the blood drained from it, leaving the hanging flesh puckered and old. "I don't like to think of it," he mumbled. " A young girl. Killed. Almost on my doorstep."

He leaned back against the cushions. Then he smiled wryly. "Good luck," he said. " I hope you get the devil who did it." 48

Paula Wunsch glanced at the diamond-encrusted wrist watch which Adolph had given her for a wedding present. Luncheon was overdue. 

She rose listlessly from her dressing table and went out into the upper hall, to the great window at its forward end which looked over the broad expanse of lawn merging into the eighteenth green. 

But her husband was nowhere in sight. Paula thought his tardiness strange. It was unlike him to break his routine for the sake of a game which he took like a dose of medicine. Perhaps something had happened. An accident. 

Paula shook her lovely head impatiently. Here she was again conjuring up silly alarms-yielding to fear. 

For she was often afraid these days. Which was odd, because she was by nature courageous. And even more ridiculous-she couldn't say what it was she feared. But for the past year she had been troubled by this constant sense of foreboding. Sometimes she thought it was caused by the hostility of the servants. Most of them had been here in the days of the first Mrs. Wunsch. 49

Through the long years of Adolph's widowerhood they had run things pretty much their own way. Naturally they resented Paula's advent. Still it was ridiculous to think they would harm her.

The truth was, her inactive monotonous life was making her neurotic. And yesterday's violent storm had completely unnerved her. Last night she had wakened, fancying again that she heard someone prowl through the house. Later she thought she heard the crash of shattered glass. It had frightened her so that she had left her bed and gone to sit at her boudoir windows to watch for the sunrise. Only when the world was bathed reassuringly in daylight, and the butler had testified that no glass was broken anywhere in the house, did her nerves become quiet. 

If only she could see people. Go places.

But travel was one of the things Adolph would not permit her. And they did almost no entertaining. His paunchy, slow-witted friends were anathema to her. 

And his rudeness to her friends amounted to a taboo.

In short, marriage had just about imprisoned her. 

Paula began to twist her wedding ring round and round her slender finger, remembering the day she had brought Adolph Wunsch to the cramped Briault apartment, to sit for an uncomfortable half hour scorched by the horror in Maman's eyes and the supercilious mockery in Truman's. 

When Wunsch had gone, Paula had faced her family spiritedly. "There's no use looking like that," she said in her clipped tones. "He's impossible. But he has millions. It's obvious I must marry him." "Has he asked you?" Maman inquired, a shade eagerly. 50

"Not yet," Paula admitted." But he will.” "One should not be too certain in such matters," Maman warned with a shiver of memory for the times her hopes of ending impoverished widowhood had been ill-founded. 

But Paula was not like her romantic American Maman. Paula was like her deeply mourned French papa. Things didn't slip through Paula's fingers. And of course the Briaults had no intention of dissuading her noble purpose. After all, a rich marriage for Paula was their goal, their only solution.

For Truman could not be counted on for anything. 

He was handsome and charming. But he had neither brains nor initiative. He was like his mother-wellmeaning, but totally unequipped for the business of living.

Naturally they looked to Paula who was so clearthinking, so resourceful and self-reliant. 

None of which stalwart qualities, Paula mused, had been any match for Adolph. The Briaults had despised him for his thick body and slow mental processes. Yet he had outwitted them all. He'd known from the start that it was his money Paula wanted. But he took her, knowing that, because, after all, it was his money, and he knew he could make the rules.

Directly after the wedding ceremony he had taken his young bride to his Beachville estate. At the end of the first week she knew exactly what to expect. By the simple expediency of not letting any of the Briaults have any cash, Adolph Wunsch made them all, virtually, his prisoners. The completeness and efficacy of his scheme dawned upon them gradually-after they were trapped-when there was nothing they could do. 51

He dealt with all of them through Paula. She was to have carte blanche as to her expenditures, he told her. 

Charge accounts had been arranged for every type of purchase except jewels and travel. For tips and other incidentals, she would receive a small cash allowance monthly. Upon Wunsch's death, she would receive ample income from a trust fund he had set up, the principal of which she could never touch and which, at her death, would go to Wunsch's daughter by his first marriage.

If Paula's mother and brother were in need of financial help, let them use what money they had to pay off their debts. They were then free to live under Wunsch's roof, and his wife was free to provide for all their material wants while they lived there. But she must never give them money, or ask her husband to do so. 

Paula had tried to show her husband, by action rather than by argument, that it was not necessary to make her his prisoner that she recognized her obligation to settle down into honest domesticity. To honor her husband before all the world. To run his home efficiently. To bear his children. She had been trained from childhood, in the French manner, for precisely that sort of program. 

But Adolph balked her. There was no reason for her to interfere with the running of the house, he said. He had a housekeeper who suited him admirably. And he wasn't keen about all this redecorating. Paula had best confine it to her boudoir.

Neither did he want children. Texie-his daughter by his first marriage-absorbed all his paternal feeling. 

He was, indeed, slavishly devoted to the willful young girl. Since she had gone abroad he had been even more 52

difficult to live with. And he had taken to going into town, night after night. He belonged to some stupid

Fraternal Organization, and he told Paula his nocturnal absences had to do with that. But she was beginning to doubt. He went so often. He invariably returned so late. He seemed so preoccupied. Was he assuaging his loneliness for Texie with a little amorous adventure?

Paula shrugged. It didn't matter to her what he did. 

Except that she dreaded being left alone in the huge, hideous house with only Truman for protection. 

And as if her thought of him had conjured his presence, she perceived her brother crossing the lawn followed by two caddies. 

How unbelievable, Paula thought, that Truman should have been playing golf all this time! For at dawn, sitting at her boudoir window, Paula had seen her brother go down the driveway carrying his golf clubs. The game was getting to be an obsession with him. And no wonder, when his activities were so limited, and a free, normal life denied him. 

Now Paula saw that Truman was dismissing the caddies. She turned back to the stairs and hurried down to meet her brother. He looked up as she spoke his name. 

Hello," he said dispiritedly. 66 

She took his arm affectionately. "Where's Adolph?" 

Truman pulled away from her. "I don't know." “But he went out on the course. Weren't you playing together?" "No." Truman moved wearily toward the stairs. 

Paula stared at him. "But-but you brought his clubs back. And his caddy," she said, confused and wondering. 53

"Because he went off with Judge Blain," Truman snapped. 66 66 

Judge Blain? What for? " 99 

My God, Paula! Will you stop asking questions?

She peered up into his face, startled. It was not like 

Truman to be disagreeable with her, and she said sharply, "What's "What's wrong?" "Just leave me alone," he said. "Will you? 99 She drew back, hurt. Then she thought, it's just this awful unnatural life he's leading. And she said quietly, "Come into the library. Let me mix you a drink.”

As she turned, she saw Follins driving the limousine under the porte-cochère and over to the road that cut across the golf course. It puzzled her momentarily that he should take that road, but she did not connect the incident with Wunsch's absence.

Truman sank down on the library divan and watched her dully as she went to the portable bar. He looked exhausted. She brought him an Old-Fashioned, and automatically he took the cherry out and gave it to her. As he used to remove it from his dessert when they were children. Her little brother. She stood looking down at him, her heart warm toward him, wanting to help him.

And Truman needed her help. He told her so, after a while, speaking tensely, utterly unlike his usual debonair self. "I've got to have money, Paula," he said. "Every cent you can get for me. I'm leaving here." 

Paula's heart leaped. "Truman! What is it? What has happened? 99 "Don't ask questions," her brother begged. "Just get the money for me." 54

"How much?" "All you can lay your hands on. I'm-I may not be back for a long time." 66 

Why? Truman, why? 99 "Will you stop that?" he demanded. "Truman, don't leave me alone here. Please don't. 

First Adolph sends Maman away. And now you are going. I can't bear it." 

66 Don't think about that,” he told her. "Think how you can get the money for me." "How soon must you have it? " 

Immediately, I tell you." "But there's only what I can save from my allowance. I gave you a hundred of that last week." “That won't take me far," he said shortly. "You must help me, Paula. You must." As she moved away from him, the butler appeared in the doorway, his moon face turned toward her.

Long after luncheon had been announced, Paula sat irresolutely in the library. 

What was troubling Truman? Why did he insist on lunching alone in his room? From what awful thing was he fleeing? 

And where was Adolph? After Truman's panic it would be a positive relief to see her husband's stolid face. She hoped that Adolph was not planning to drive into New York tonight. If he were, she would swallow her pride and beg him to stay with her. 

She rose, coming to sudden decision. Better go in to luncheon now. The servants would be wondering. But she paused with her hand on the doorknob. The loneliness. And the fear. She felt she would welcome even the presence of Grossvater Wunsch, though his table manners distressed her beyond words, and he seldom spoke to her.

But he lunched always in his own rooms, appearing 56

downstairs occasionally for dinner. Perhaps he would lunch with her if she asked him, Paula thought. And moved by impulse she went up to the old man's sittingroom and knocked softly. 

Miss Doty opened the door, her hair gleaming redgold, like fire over the snowy white of her uniform. She stood aside silently when she saw Paula, and Paula moved past the nurse without greeting. There was an atmosphere of constraint between the two young women. 

Grossvater Wunsch was sitting on the window seat, his ponderous Bible on his knees, its heavy German script much underscored. When he saw his daughterin-law, he laid the Book aside and stood before her. 

Though he was past eighty, there was still some remnant of the grandeur of his manhood. He had been a giant in his prime-six feet four, with massive shoulders and arms, and barrel-chested. His head was large and well-shaped, and the thick growth of white hair framed it in patriarchal dignity. He had not lost his direct gaze, nor a certain vigor of aspect, but Paula knew these cloaked weakness. For he had aged rapidly in the three years she had lived under the same roof with him. He was quickly exhausted and the victim of recurrent heart attacks. Wunsch, deeply solicitous for his father, had long ago installed the trained nurse to be in constant attendance upon him. And lately, as if he resented any contemplation of his fast-waning powers, the old man shut himself more and more within his rooms, alone with the nurse, waiting in dignity and silence for the coming of death.

Paula spoke slowly and distinctly in answer to his look of inquiry. "Will you lunch downstairs with me?" 

She added quickly, "I am all alone.” 57

Perhaps he saw the wistfulness deep in her eyes, for something made him check his immediate refusal. He looked at her searchingly. "Ja wohl," he said abruptly in his husky guttural tones.

He went into his bedroom, followed by Miss Doty who helped him change his dressing gown for a loose jacket. Then he rejoined Paula, his cane grasped firmly in his gnarled old hand as he followed her stiffly down the stairs and into the dining-room. 

The butler signalled the footmen with a flicker of his eyelids, and the smooth elaborate service of luncheon was under way.

Paula studied the old man, attacking his food with a primitive fierceness. Did he wonder sadly why he had let his rich son transplant him to this foreign country, to this unsuitable luxury? There was something pathetic about the way he treasured the few possessions he had brought from his Austrian farm-his Bible, his cane, a few faded photographs of people long dead, some snapshots of fish he had caught, game he had killed.

Now the hands which had been quick and strong and skilled on plow, reel, and trigger, shook as they hovered over the plate and wavered to his mouth, spilling food. 

But he seemed oblivious to these mishaps, neglecting the use of his napkin, wielding his knife freely. 

Paula shuddered. Then she chided herself, and spoke to him gently. "What do you hear from Texie? "

He lifted his head sharply at the name of his granddaughter. "Where is Texie?" he demanded.

She told him patiently, "Texie is travelling in

Maman is with her."

Nothing of her reactions or behavior. Which was a pity. Because Texie's reactions and behavior, whatever else you might say about them, were never dull, and always plentiful. Of course Adolph heard from Texie 59

regularly, Paula knew. But, no doubt because of Truman, he never discussed his daughter's letters with his wife.

Yes, it might well be, Paula thought with deepening insight, that Wunsch's increasing restlessness, his bad temper, and his recent addiction to night life, were caused by Texie's deliberately absenting herself in defiance of him.

Paula roused from her musing as Grossvater pushed his plate away petulantly, grasped his cane, and shoved back his chair. The butler hastened to his assistance, and the old man struggled to his feet and stood for a moment finding his balance.

Paula watched him blankly. There were still salad and dessert courses to come. Then she thought, why should she linger for them? She too was weary of the solemn luncheon ceremony. She overtook the old man and slipped her arm through his. "Come up to my boudoir," she said coaxingly, "and visit."

She knew that he was almost inarticulate, but he was not stupid. He had lived a long time. There was wisdom stored behind his sharp eyes. If she could get him to talk to her from his heart, from the pinnacle of his years, perhaps he would show her the way out of the cul-de-sac into which she had strayed. 

But he disengaged himself firmly. "Nein,” he said. "I go rest now. now."

She went with him to the door of his sitting-room. He turned on the threshold and regarded her strangely. "Life iss hard on all," he said unexpectedly. " Iss only for us to do the Lord's Will.”

She turned back to the stairs feeling bleak and 60

empty. The Lord's Will? There were so many and such markedly personal interpretations of that. How was one to know which way Truth lay?

With her hand on the balustrade, she paused, hearing voices in the hall below. Her husband and the chauffeur. As she appeared on the landing, Follins saluted and withdrew. Wunsch turned to his wife. He looked old and weary as she had never seen him before.

"I'll speak as I please," Wunsch told her contemptuously. 

Paula's fear and anxiety found relief in anger. "Then so will I." She eyed him levelly. "I do not believe for one moment that it is to Committee meetings that you go, night after night, leaving me alone."

For a time Paula stood there, alone on the landing. 

Tears of anger sparkled in her dark eyes. He was lying to her. And he did not care that she knew it. In France, if a husband chose to be unfaithful, he managed the matter with finesse. But Adolph saw no need for finesse. 

Paula had married him for money. He had bought her. 

He owned her. And he would treat her as he saw fit. 

There must be something she could do. She had brains. Now was the time to use them. She dried her eyes and went thoughtfully downstairs and into the library. 

She closed the door into the hall and locked it. Went to the windows and shut them, adjusting the Venetian blinds so that no one could look into the room from the terrace.

Then she took the telephone directories from the bottom drawer of the desk. The Classified would be quickest. 

Under the heading DETECTIVE AGENCIES her finger trailed down the list of names, and came to rest against one she recognized. She put the directories back in the drawer. Then she put the call through. The connection was established, and she spoke clearly and concisely. "This is Mrs. Adolph Wunsch. I am calling from 63

my home in Beachville. My husband will leave here about seven o'clock this evening, in a Rolls limousine. 

He is to be driven to New York. I want him followed." 

But it was necessary that such a matter be arranged by a personal call at the office, she was told. There wasn't time, she objected sharply. This was an emergency. If this agency was not interested, she could go elsewhere. If they were interested, she would come to town tomorrow with ample cash for expenses and fees. 

She went on to give exact description of the location of the estate, of her husband, of his chauffeur, of the point at which the car would emerge on the highway. 

When it was all arranged, she put the telephone in place and lit a cigarette. She sat watching its glowing ember, thinking, "How calm I am. I feel nothing."

But at that the constraint she had placed upon herself vanished. Without knowing it, she rose and looked about her as though she had just wakened from sleepwalking. She felt wretched and confused. What had she done? Set a spy to track her husband. A horrible, ugly thing for a wife to do.

Her hand was on the telephone when reaction set in. 

She recalled the scene on the landing-all the indignities her husband had heaped on her mother and brother. Her face hardened. Let things stay as they were. Adolph deserved this. Find out to what woman he went. Get a divorce. And alimony. Heavy alimony.

Make him pay for denying her wifehood in all respects save one.

She went to the bar, poured some whiskey into a glass, and drank it. That steadied her somewhat. But the next moment her thoughts were milling frantically.

Suppose the detective reported that her husband really 64

was at a Committee meeting? Suppose Adolph found out then what she had done? He'd throw her out. How would she look after Truman and Maman then?

She took a book at random from the shelves, sat on the divan and forced herself to read, comprehending nothing.

A long time after, she heard her husband's voice in the hall. She glanced at her wrist watch. Almost seven. 

Adolph was leaving. And he was calling her. "Paula. Paula."

She did not answer. She could not face him. Not after what she had done to him. 

And then he was gone. She could hear the double slamming of the house and car doors, the quickened purr of the motor and the swish of tires turning on gravel.

Her imagination winged after the car, went with it down the long curving driveway, past the golf course, through the patch of woodland where deer moved shadowy, down the avenue of magnificent copper beeches, under the entrance arch, and out on the highway.

Then another car would slink forth and, steadily rolling the miles away, the two cars would travel on and on, one leading, the other trailing. Where would they go? What would she learn from the detective who rode in that second car?

Unconsciously she had moved to the telephone, and suddenly her arms stretched out across the desk and her head went down on them and she sobbed-the terrible, heartbroken sobs of a woman who is lost and knows not where to turn.

After a while she quieted, her emotion exhausted. 65

She went back mechanically to her book and picked it up. Then she realized she should be dressing for dinner. 

She must go through that ordeal because of the servants. It wouldn't do to have things seem at all unusual. 

Adolph might get a report.

She composed herself as best she could. Dabbed at her eyes with the wet ball of her handkerchief, and threw it aside to smooth her hair. Without mirror or powder, there wasn't much she could do, she thought dully. She must try to slip upstairs without anyone observing her.

She went to the door-listened-turned the key carefully. Opened the door. And stood face to face with a man she had never seen before. 66

Madden. They'd best drive Doyle to Wunsch's house and let the detective proceed with the investigation in his own way.

During the drive Doyle had several questions to ask. 

Blain answered them concisely and positively. He could swear to it, the Judge stated flatly, that there'd been no mysterious disappearance of any young woman from Beachville or the entire County in which it was situated. 

Madden zestfully corroborated this. What went on among the residents, as well as the shifting summer population, it was evident these two men would know in detail-as only natives of a small town, feeding own life, can know the lives of those about them. The least whisper of any irregularity would come to their 69

attention, they assured Doyle. Certainly no one in that vicinity could be blacked out completely, and not have inquiry and hullabaloo sweep the town.

But on a big estate like Wunsch's-Doyle pressed-a sort of township in itself?

Blain shrugged. The Wunsches had darn little privacy. Anything that concerned the town's millionaire concerned the town. 

And Madden recalled, with a chuckle, that when 

Wunsch decided to give his daughter, Texie, a European tour, Oshansky, the village stationer, doubled his stock of city newspapers and sold out every last one and 'could have sold more, on account of the pictures of Texie and Mrs. Wunsch's mother on board the steamer. 

So much for the Wunsch family, Doyle agreed. What about their house guests, their visitors, and the people who worked on the estate? Could the natives keep track of all those?

That might be another story, Blain admitted. Still, lots of the Village folks had seasonal work of one kind or another on the Wunsch place, and naturally enough they kept their eyes and ears open and their tongues wagging. 

How about it, Doyle asked? Was Wunsch well liked?

He'd been very generous, Blain said evasively. He'd built the High School gymnasium and cafeteria. And of course he was the big taxpayer of the district. The town couldn't very well get along without him. 

Madden was more outspoken. It was evident he still smarted from Wunsch's contemptuous treatment. Somebody ought to get after that guy with a horsewhip, if you asked Madden. Hitting the high spots. When he had everything at home that a man could want. The 70

second Mrs. Wunsch was a young and beautiful woman.

Menjou. Who'd know better than Madden, directing traffic seven nights a week, how often Wunsch was driven into New York in that big Rolls of his? Because Wunsch came rolling home long after traffic lights were out and traffic cops decently in bed.

The Wunsch house was a large white marble edifice. 

Its elaborate approach of steps and terraces, its central bulk, mostly entrance, and its side wings, mostly windows, made Doyle think of the Public Library in New York. Two stone lions crouching on pedestals increased the illusion. But thank God, Doyle noted, there were no messy pigeons.

Th he butler was admirably impassive when Doyle reappeared at the door, this time demanding Mrs.

He indicated the least comfortable of the hall chairs for Doyle to sit upon, and withdrew to some mysterious lurking place from which he popped occasionally, to see what the odd visitor was up to.

Midway of the hall, the staircase mounted in a magnificent curve. Coming toward the entrance doors, arches opened on the right, into the drawing-room-a big, square apartment, obviously decorated and furnished to Wunsch's taste with, Doyle marvelled, chairs big enough for elephants to sit in, and more paint on the thick gold frames than on the pictures themselves. 

Beyond the drawing-room he could see the conservatory and the dining hall. They must have placed the massive dining furniture where it stood and then built the room around it, he thought. 

He turned back into the hall. To his left there was a closed door, and he strove against temptation to open it. Mustn't get too snoopy, he admonished himself.

With one eye cocked for the advent of the butler, he strolled to the door, inclined his ear to the keyhole, and heard nothing. An empty room, obviously.

Another room behind the stairs he did not venture to investigate, for it was too close to the butler's lair. He sat down again watching the approach from the golf course, figuring Mrs. Wunsch would come that way.

What the hell? Did everyone in this cockeyed house lock himself in? The doorknob revolved. 

The next moment the door opened, and Doyle stood face to face with one of the most beautiful women he had ever seen. Her beauty was not lessened by the obvious fact that she had been weeping. cs Th he next morning Truman Briault slept heavily, exhausted by a wakeful night of fear and worry. And farther down the corridor, in the master bedroom, Adolph Wunsch slumbered like a man who had been drugged-worn out from a night's activities which had not brought him home until dawn.

But Paula was up early-braced for a difficult day and the things she must do to combat its difficulties.

He watched her quick haughty departure, muttering to himself. These women. The airs of them. And they'd do anythinganything to get their hands on money. Men were fools not to see that. 

Paula drove to the Detective Agency she had telephoned the day before. As she battled with the traffic, her humiliation over the episode with the couturier flamed into fury against her husband. How dare he force her into such a position? His servants were well paid for their contributions to his comfort. But his wife rated only room and board and clothing. If she needed money she had to contrive by low means to get it. 96

Her angry mood steadied her throughout the interview with the Chief of the Agency. She paid over to him some of the money she had acquired. He gave her, in return, a typed report of her husband's movements of the night before. She studied the report, frowning over it as though it puzzled her. "This address on Amsterdam Avenue-that is an apartment house?" she asked.

The Chief nodded. 

"But you do not know whom my husband visits there?" "Not yet. 99 She opened her bag again, took out several bills and laid them on the desk. "Have my husband followed every night he comes into town," she ordered. "Do not send the reports to me. I'll phone or come for them."

The thrust struck home. Wunsch colored angrily, and his eyes shifted from her direct gaze. He said nothing more.

Truman was equally silent. It smote Paula to see his white desperate face. Perhaps it was only her fancy, but he seemed actually to have lost weight in the few hours since she had seen him stealing out into the dawn, carrying his golf clubs-setting off on God alone knew what awful errand.

She must talk to him-must get to the bottom of his trouble. Last night she had learned nothing. He would not even admit her to his room. She had to slip a note under his door telling him she had a message for him which no one must overhear. Only then did he let her in to see that his bags were packed, and that he lingered only for nightfall to hide his departure. 

But the terror the detective, Doyle, had put into her-the explicit, ominous warning he had sent to Truman through her had their effect. Truman was still afraid to stay-she could see that-but he was now more afraid to go. 

What did he fear? Her mind twisted and delved and groped. She must find some way to be alone with her brother-some private place where she could persuade him to unburden his troubled heart. 

She spoke casually. "We have a golf date for this afternoon, Truman." 

As he looked at her in surprise, she caught his eye warningly. "Don't tell me you have forgotten?" 

After a moment he said dully, " I haven't forgotten.”

Directly luncheon was over, she ran upstairs to change into golf shoes. When she came down into the hall she saw with relief that her husband was shutting 98

himself into his study-a little room behind the stairs which he kept locked, and in which he spent hours going over his complicated records. 

Truman was waiting for her out on the terrace. She slipped her arm through his. The caddy was already carrying their bags to the first tee. "We must get rid of that boy," she murmured. "Whatever I do or say, follow my lead." 

Doyle, like Paula, was up bright and early that morning, likewise regarding the day as full of things to be done.

But his plans received a temporary delay from Ellen. 

For Ellen scarcely touched her breakfast what with watching her husband hurry through his. And when, at the last mouthful, he was on his feet and making for the street, Ellen folded her napkin with decision and was up and after him. She stalked by his side, silent until they were out of earshot of the refined boarders, interminably rocking themselves on the porches of the 

Seaview. Then she let him have it.

What did he think he was up to anyway? This was three days in succession he'd gone off, returning just in the nick of time for dinner, with explanations and excuses that were insults to a wife's intelligence. For all the years of their marriage she'd always bragged what a fine, steady man he was. But if he had any notion that now he had come to what was known as the Dangerous Age, he'd find out that Ellen could give him more danger than any snakey husband-snatcher. 

There was only one thing to do. Doyle swore her to secrecy and told her about the skeleton. She thought he was crazy, she said bitterly and with variations, 99

spending his lovely vacation hard at work on the same kind of mess he had the year round. But she let him go then, and stood fondly gazing after him. What a man!

The Saints be praised he was hers.

Pay Station. 

Certainly, Dr. Blake said. His telephone would be available to Doyle at any time. And he led the detective into his laboratory.

It would take time to go through the records, so he suggested that the jeweler call him back and reverse the charges. 66 I'll see to it that you collect for these calls from the Village," he told Dr. Blake. I think I have something for you. A clue." And he told Doyle that the skeleton had a complete and perfect set of teeth. "Do you expect me to cheer over that?" Doyle grumbled. remarkable? That girl must have had a beautiful smile." "You've got a better imagination than I have." 

Doyle grimaced at the grinning death's head. 

The telephone rang.

The jeweler had the desired information. Mr. 

Wunsch had purchased a diamond wrist watch three years ago on the first day of June, for Mrs. Wunsch, whom he married later that month. But there had never been any sale of a duplicate clasp. However, Mr. Doyle might be interested to know that, thirteen months after the purchase of the watch, Mr. Wunsch had ordered a duplicate watch. 

Doyle whistled in surprise. Was the second watch monogrammed?

Neither of them was. The design did not permit. 

Did the jeweler know, then, for whom Mr. Wunsch had bought the second watch?

He did not. And, as it happened, he had personally dealt with Mr. Wunsch on that second purchase. 

Because the watches were exceedingly valuable-not regular stock, by any means. It had been necessary to make the second watch to order. And that had required several weeks. The delay had annoyed Mr. Wunsch. 

Doyle asked another question. Did Wunsch ever bring anyone with him to the jeweler's during this transaction? 

No. And he had said nothing which would throw light on the identity of the recipient of the second watch. 

The jeweler had rather taken it for granted that Mrs. 

Wunsch had lost her watch and that the duplicate was intended for her. But he had been careful to make no inquiry. A gentleman's purchase of valuable jewelry to 101

give to a lady was a delicate subject for conversation. 

A jeweler who did not sense when to be discreet and uncurious would lose his best customers. 

Doyle put the receiver down and turned to Dr. Blake. "Want to go raking leaves in the woods?" he inquired. "In this heat?" The doctor grunted. "Like hell I do." 

So Doyle went to Judge Blain's house.

The Judge, at work in the barn, viewed the detective's arrival with noticeable lack of enthusiasm, and took immediate occasion to announce that he was busy as all get-out. But when he heard what Doyle had learned, he grabbed a couple of rakes, threw them in the car and piled in after them. 

As they drove toward Wunsch's woodland, Doyle elaborated his theory.

The diamond wrist watch worn by the murdered girl had been bound to her wrist by a silver ribbon, just as Mrs. Wunsch's was. After the murder, the ribbon had rotted, and the watch and the clasp had dropped from the bones of the wrist into the leaves. The watch, being heavier, had buried itself. The paper-thin clasp had remained in view. If Doyle had had the wits he was born with, he'd have recognized that clasp for what it was when he picked it up, and then and there searched for the watch.

The watch would be a valuable find. Someone, somewhere, would remember seeing it on the wrist of the young girl who had worn it to her death. Perhaps she wore other jewelry, too. If they found jewelry, they'd know at least that robbery was no motive for the crime. 102

The two men raked painstakingly, alert for the clink of their rakes against the metal of the watch. They worked, fortunately, in the shade, but the still heat of the day enveloped them and they were tormented by mosquitoes. As they slapped and scratched, Doyle swore lustily, and the more pious Judge called feelingly upon a series of great names-Jehovah, Scott, and Guns. But always new recruits arrived and attacked. "If I had Wunsch's millions," Doyle said, resting on his rake, "I'd get rid of these damn pests." 

The Judge shook his head. "Don't suppose Wunsch knows they're here," he argued. "Don't suppose he ever sets foot on this end of the property, now he's quit going to the brewery." 

Doyle stared mean 99 at him. "The brewery? You 

Blain nodded. "Sure. Wunsch started business here." "Is the brewery in operation now?" "Not for years. When the well began to run dry in this brewery, Wunsch built the big plant over in New Jersey. His beer never amounted to very much after that." 

Doyle grinned. "Boost Beachville," he commented. "Nothing of the sort," Blain denied. "I don't drink the stuff myself. But it was common talk that his beer lost its quality when he made that move. They say the flavor of a brew is very dependent on a plentiful supply of fresh, sweet water. It wasn't Wunsch's expensive equipment—it was the well in the old brewery that made his beer famous." "Is the brewery still standing?" "Sure. Wunsch was sentimental about it. Used to visit it every day until it got spoiled for him." 103

Doyle scratched a mosquito bite vigorously. "How the hell can a brewery get spoiled?" 66 "The Federal Agents just about tore it apart,"

Doyle gave it hasty inspection. "There should be another one like it. High heels."

Presently all the leaves in the section where the skeleton had lain were raked back and banked around a cleared center. But there was no sign of the watch. "What do you make of that?" Blain asked.

Doyle said grimly, " Briault found it when he moved the skeleton." "Maybe not," the Judge contradicted. "Likely it's pretty small. Might have slipped between the teeth of the rake."

He got down on his knees and began to sift the dried dead leaves through his fingers. Doyle went to his assistance. An hour passed.

And then, as a sense of futility began to weigh heavily within each, Doyle's hopes revived. His fingers groped nervously. Most likely just a pebble, he told

Blain. But he delved cautiously into the crackling leaves. Blain squatted back on his heels, waiting and watching as Doyle rose to his feet. "Got it?" the Judge asked. "Not what we're looking for." Doyle's tone was absorbed. He rolled something between his fingers and grinned at Blain. "But maybe this is just as good," he said. "The watch might tell us who was murdered. But this should lead us to the murderer."

Blain held out his hand for the distorted bullet which had pierced the young girl's skull. 

His eyes narrowed as he examined it. "I'd say .44 calibre," he announced. "Tell you what I'd like to do.

Ballistics is kind of a hobby of mine. Let me take this home and study it under the microscope." "O.K.," Doyle agreed heartily. "That's quicker than sending to the City for a report." 105

They went back to their search of the leaves but with no further results. Doyle berated himself harshly for letting the watch escape him, when he'd been so close to it picking up the clasp. 

The Judge agreed gloomily that was a bad break. 

Because for all they could tell now, maybe there had been more jewelry scattered in the leaves around the body. Maybe Briault took it all. Or maybe he took nothing-not even the watch. Trouble was, this way they'd never know whether or not the motive for the murder was robbery. 

Doyle said he was beginning to think maybe it was.

He thought it queer that they had found no metal handbag frame. Nowadays no woman went any distance without carting a bagful of junk-cosmetics, cigarettes, and so on. And it didn't seem reasonable that a young woman who'd hold Wunsch up for one expensive gift of jewelry would stop there. 

Blain suggested they could figure three ways. Maybe the motive was robbery, and so a handbag and jewelry were stolen. Or maybe the murderer killed for some other reason and took those things to prevent identification of the body. Or maybe they were all there, covered up by the leaves, and Briault found them and took them. 

Doyle rubbed his chin. "It's up to me to get the answer to those three possibilities,” he said. “I'm going to grill that guy Briault, if I have to break his door down to get to him." 

So the Judge went home to lunch, and Doyle had a quick hamburger at Krimp's, and then came back along the woodland path on the short cut to the Wunsch house. His jaw was set and his thoughts were busy. He 106

was anxious to have things out with Briault. And his opportunity came in a way he had not anticipated. Doyle had emerged from the woods and was skirting the thirteenth green when a golf ball struck almost at his feet. A moment later, a second ball thudded on the green and rolled in a direct line for the cup, stopping just short of it. It was an approach to gladden any golfer's heart, but Doyle wasted no time in admiration of it. The players were still hidden from view by the hilly countryside, as the detective wheeled and darted for the cover of the woods. Two balls meant two players. 

And two players meant conversation. And hearing things he wasn't meant to hear had solved many a case for many a detective.

He had chosen his hiding place at random, making for the spot where the shrubbery looked thickest. The girl embodied some menace for Wunsch. Either she threatened his domestic happiness or his public reputation. So Wunsch killed her. 

Mrs. Wunsch had not been unaware of the affair. At least she had known of her husband's gift of the watch similar to her own. The terrific shock of this realization had brought on her sudden illness.

Lying with closed eyes, Paula was dimly aware of a discussion going on in hushed tones.

Her husband was insisting that he get an extra nurse to care for his wife. But Miss Doty argued him down. "You'd be making a serious mistake," she said earnestly. "You'll make Mrs. Wunsch think she's very 133

One that she could easily and gladly carry, the nurse responded. 

Paula felt ashamed as she listened to the warmth and generosity of Miss Doty's speech. Why had she believed the nurse was hostile to her? The truth was, she had believed that everyone who worked under her roof was hostile to her. Now she knew she was as wrong in her estimate of all of them as she had been of Miss Doty. 

Oddly enough, Paula felt at peace now that her vague presentiments of tragedy had crystallized into facts. Now she could see that her long fear of impending horror had indeed been justified, but that her interpretation of this fear had been in error. She had looked for trouble from every source but the correct one. She had suspected and misjudged everyone about her, and been blind to the danger threatening so close at hand. 

Her thoughts went fumblingly over the developments of the afternoon, from the moment the detective had come from the woods to interrupt her talk with Truman. 

How much of that talk had Doyle heard? What deductions would he draw from it? After all, why should she be so afraid? She had a right to plan to go to Paris for her brother's wedding.

At this point her mind lost some of its haziness and she recalled Truman's violent opposition to her plans. 

The recollection was like an icy hand at her heart. The detective had heard Truman's protests. And the detective had found the watch. 134
