Some hours later she awoke. Her mind felt surprisingly clear and cool and comfortable. She knew everything that had happened. And what she must do about it. The first thing to do was to lie there quietly and plan how to get in touch with Doyle. 136

When she had thought that through, she opened her eyes cautiously. Under the night light her Dresden clock said 1:20. Through the wide-flung French windows, the sounds of the summer night came to hercrickets and katydids droning away. She was alone in the room.

She sat up in bed carefully, for she was very weak. 

She must do what she had to do quickly-while the household slept, before her husband came home, before her strength was exhausted. 

She remembered Doyle saying he was vacationing at the Seaview. Thank God she could reach the telephone from the day bed. She sometimes wondered why she had insisted on having a telephone connection in her boudoir. Because nobody ever called her any more. But now that didn't matter. Nothing mattered except that in this hour of crisis when she had desperate need of a telephone, one was there to her hand. Almost as if, long ago, she had known of this night and prepared for it.

It seemed an unendurable wait after she lifted the phone before the operator spoke to her sleepily. "The Seaview Hotel,” Paula said. "I want to speak to Mr. Patrick Doyle."

She could hear the monotonous buzzing for the connection and she waited tensely, going over what she would say. She would tell Doyle he must insist on seeing her alone. Maybe she ought to phone Dr. Blake, too, and explain to him. She leaned back against the pillows and closed her eyes, conserving her energy while she waited.

were opened wide to the black night. And through them she seemed to see some formless mass, blacker than the night. 

It moved.

and buried itself in the plaster. Doyle dug it out with his pocket knife and put it in his wallet to give to Judge Blain. A cursory examination suggested that it was strikingly similar to the bullet they had found in the woods.

Doyle went over to the windows. They were of the 

French type three of them adjoining, the double doors of each opened wide to admit the sultry night air and to give access to the balcony. Each window was screened by an almost invisible length of copper wire, which rolled up from the floor like a window shade. 

The screening of the center window showed a jagged tear where the bullet had come through.

Doyle stepped out on the balcony through one of the end windows, pulling a lighted floor lamp after him as far as its cord would allow. He angled the light on the bullet hole in the center window screening. But there were no shreds of cloth or other revealing matter on the ragged ends of wire where the barrel of a gun had been pressed. Nor were there any apparent fingerprints on the window framing, nor footprints on the marble floor.

Doyle took his stand behind the center window, stuck his finger through the punctured screening and sighted along it. He had an unobstructed view of Paula lying on the day bed, and he could draw a nice bead on Miss 

Doty bending over her. It was surprising that the shot had missed its target. Mrs. Wunsch's scream must have come just as the murderer pulled the trigger, causing him to deflect his aim toward the right. 

The detective adjusted the lamplight to get an idea of the extent of the balcony. He was disheartened to see that it extended right and left from Mrs. Wunsch's 147

boudoir windows, the entire rear length of the house, overlooking the gardens.

He moved at random to the left and passed under unlighted casement windows. Then a group of three 

French windows opened into a bedroom, in which he could see a night lamp burning, and one of a pair of beds turned down, but not slept in. The Wunsches' bedroom, Doyle surmised.

Now more casement windows-these lighted. He decided that the casement windows, all placed above eye level, lighted bath- and dressing-rooms. French windows again, lighted, opening into the room with leather chairs where Wunsch had received him yesterday. 

That brought him to the end of the balcony. He leaned over the balustrade and peered around the angle of the outer wall, looking down on to the glass roof of the conservatory. There was a heavy copper leader running from roof to ground. Doyle regarded it gloomily. As if there weren't enough exits and entrances to the damn balcony without this possibility. 

Passing Paula's boudoir windows to the right, he came upon a series of three French windows which were dark, closed, and locked. Then casement windows, also dark. Three more French windows, dark and locked. 

More casements, lighted. Three French windows, open and lighted, showing a bed-sitting-room, masculine in aspect. Briault's room.

Doyle went back and checked all the open French windows. Every one was screened, and the screens could not be manipulated from the balcony. Not that this meant much. No more than the locked windows did. For it would not be difficult for the murderer to raise a screen or unlock a window, step out on the balcony, 148

take a shot at Mrs. Wunsch, run back to the room he had emerged from, lock the window and lower the screen. 

Then what? Why then he'd hide the gun, of course.

And rush out into the hall. Look wildly around. Inquire of everyone" Was that a shot? Where? From Mrs. 

Wunsch's room? Quick. Find out if she is safe."

It developed that the latter part of Doyle's fancies were a fairly exact résumé of what everyone in the house had done and said. For Doyle insisted on questioning them all that night, over Wunsch's bitter protests. "It's three o'clock in the morning," Wunsch said, apparently astounded at Doyle's insistence. "Please don't talk in here," Miss Doty whispered, glancing uneasily at Paula, asleep at last. The nurse manœuvred the two men toward the door and out into the hall.

Here the servants were still congregated; and Dr. 

Blake stood talking to Briault, assuring him patiently that Paula had not been so much as scratched. "What can you possibly learn at this hour that you can't learn just as well in the morning?" The house servants slept on the third floor. One by one they testified to being awakened, badly scared, by the sound of the shot, not knowing from where it came, venturing in a body down the service stairs to the second floor, where they heard voices and movement. 

Mr. Briault's room was at the end of the corridor nearest the service stairs. They all agreed that his door stood open and that his room was brightly lighted.

He had been reading in bed when he heard the shot.

Dark red would look black out on the dark balcony. 156

The second floor hall was T-shaped. At the head of the curving marble staircase, a long corridor stretched right and left to form the crosspiece of the T. It was into this corridor that doors opened from Wunsch's sitting-room, the master bedroom, Paula's boudoir, an unoccupied suite which Miss Doty identified as Texie's, and Truman Briault's apartment. At the far right end of the corridor, just beyond Briault's room, a door opened on to the service stairway. 

Flanking the stem of the T to the left, the old man's three-room suite faced, across the stair well, the three guest rooms.

The forward end of the hall was glass-a magnificent leaded window framing a wide vista of lawn and park, golf course and Bay.

Doyle made a cursory inspection of the guest rooms, but they revealed nothing of interest. There were no signs of recent occupancy, and there was no access to the balcony from them, except by crossing the hall and passing through one of the rooms opening on the long corridor.

Miss Doty assured him the same was true of the elder Wunsch's suite, which included her room. But despite her violent whispered objections that he would disturb the old man, Doyle insisted on seeing for himself.

In the next quarter hour, she made three of these brief trips, while Doyle watched, frowning and puzzled. But his brow smoothed when she emerged the third time with a cup of steaming coffee.

He tiptoed to the door and whispered, "Got some for me? "

She was so startled that she nearly dropped the cup. "Haven't you got any sense?" she inquired with feeling. She gave him a disgusted look, but went back to her room and returned with two cups. "I'm a fool to give you any," she said bitterly, handing him one of them.

He waved her off to her post and went back to his own. The hot coffee hit the right spot. Miss Doty was a bright girl to think of it. He could see her across the hall, putting her empty cup aside and yawning. Doyle yawned in sympathy.

The house was weirdly quiet. Without sound or movement it waited for the dawn. Soon milky light seeped into Maman's room. Wouldn't anything happen now, Doyle thought, yawning again. Still it wouldn't do to relax. He sat erect in his chair and planted his feet determinedly. But sleep was heavy within him. 

He was watching Miss Doty's nodding head and thinking that she wouldn't be able to hold out much longer, when he drifted off into heavy slumber. 166

Doyle said, "You're damn plausible, sister. But you're talking through your hat.” 66 She gave him a look of utter exasperation and drifted toward the door. "If you want proof that coffee was queer,” she said, “ just take a look at yourself in the mirror. Or maybe I'm wrong," she corrected sweetly. "Maybe that dopey look is natural.”

But Doyle did not need a mirror to tell him that he had known brighter mornings than this. His heavy sleep had left him strangely unrefreshed, and his head was buzzing oddly. He went into Maman's bathroom and treated himself to a shower. 

He thought Miss Doty's suspicions very astute, though he had been at such pains to scoff at her for them. Because he did not want to put her and the entire household into a panic. The insidious fear of drugged food and drink, of possible death by poison, would disrupt the establishment and release a flood of unbased rumors and imaginary dangers which would make Doyle's difficult task more difficult. Sometime during the day he'd manage to slip into Miss Doty's room and get some of that coffee for analysis. 

Feeling nearer normal after his bath, he went in search of the housekeeper to demand the key to Texie's door. 

The woman was astounded at his request. "That door isn't locked," she said. "It must be stuck. This damp sea air, you know."

But Doyle convinced her by practical demonstration that damp sea air had nothing to do with Texie's door. 

Still murmuring incredulity, she sought the key among 170

her jingling collection, inserted it in the lock and turned it. Then she preceded Doyle into the apartment, looking suspiciously about her. Her manner made it clear that skeletons in the woods and shots in the night could not throw her off balance. The house was her province and nobody had better try any tricks in it. 

Doyle said, "If things don't look right to you, be sure you tell me."

She finished her survey of the sitting-room without comment, and passed into the dressing-room, which in turn opened into the bedroom. The result of this inspection was a disappointment to Doyle. For all the rooms had that strange hush, that unnatural neatness of detail, that patient waiting air of the very furniture, which bespeaks the long absence of an occupant. 

The detective wandered back into the sitting-room, the housekeeper alertly treading on his heels, watching uneasily as his square-toed shoes trod heavily over the white velour carpeting. It was apparent that she regarded Doyle's every move as a potential menace to the ultra-modern white and silver décor.

He came to a stop beside the suite's one incongruity -a low divan covered in black muslin. "What's the idea of that?" 

She sighed at his obtuseness. "That's a dust cover."

Her head wagged disapproval. " That couch is covered in silver brocade, no less. We put black muslin over it to keep the silver threads from tarnishing." She peered down at the divan. Then her hand shot out. "Now look at that!" "Don't touch anything," Doyle warned. “I must,” she snapped, and lifted a corner of the dust cover. 171

Doyle brushed her hand aside none too gently. "What's wrong?" "You can see for yourself," she told him tartly. "The muslin isn't tucked down in back. It has to cover the whole thing or it doesn't do a bit of good.” Her tongue clicked against the roof of her mouth. "These maids."

Doyle removed the dust cover carefully, and disclosed the divan, spotless, smooth, and untarnished. But two of the four cushions lying on it were out of alignment, their lower edges rumpled as if something had been thrust under them. Doyle lifted them cautiously. 

Beneath them he saw a faint impress where something had borne down against the soft upholstery and left a curving mark and below it a straight, short line. The straight line could have been made by the barrel of a gun. The curving line Doyle was at a loss to account for. It curved too deeply for the butt of a revolver. 

He addressed the housekeeper abruptly. "Give me the keys to this suite. No one is to come in here." 

She took a look at the set of his jaw and decided against protest. While she stripped the keys from her ring, Doyle examined the windows. In the sitting-room and bedroom he found the screens drawn and, behind them, the French doors securely locked from the inside. 

The casement windows in the dressing-room were also locked, but to get at them Doyle had to move a bed which was crowded in beneath them. Pushing the bed back into place it suddenly struck him as odd that it should be there. It was obviously one of a pair, the second of which stood alone in the spacious bedroom. 

He called to the housekeeper, "What's the idea of the bed in the dressing-room? 99 172

She told him, handing him the keys, "Miss Texie ordered that bed moved into the dressing-room the last week she was home." "Then it used to be in the bedroom?" "Yes." 66 

Why did she move it out? 99 "I don't know. I suppose they must have quarrelled.” 

Doyle stared at her. "Who must have quarrelled?” "Miss Texie and Helen Voss."

He spoke idly to cover the leap of keen interest which the mention of her name brought, "Who is 

Helen Voss? "

The housekeeper sniffed. "I don't quite know how to answer that. One of the family, you might say." "What would she and Texie quarrel about?" Doyle asked. "A man? 99 But at that the woman's lean jaw set and her eyes lowered. "How would I know?" she muttered, and added hastily that she must get to her work. She left abruptly with a firm tread which sent the flat crystal pendants of the lighting fixtures into a tinkling dance. 

Doyle sat on a white satin chair, and took time off to construct a theory of what had happened the night before. 

Sometime prior to 1:30 A. M.-which was when

Mrs. Wunsch had telephoned him-someone had entered 

Texie's suite, gone out on the balcony, watched and waited until Miss Doty left the boudoir, fired at Mrs. 

Wunsch, ran back into Texie's sitting-room and hidden the gun in the divan. 

Then, later, finding Miss Doty and Doyle conveniently drugged by the coffee, someone had reentered Texie's room and removed the gun. 173

The weak spot in this reconstruction, however, was the interval between the hiding of the gun and its removal.

Had the murderer remained in the locked room, risking the chance of discovery if an entrance were forced? 

Or had he come out into the corridor to mix with those who gathered there?

Doyle reasoned that it was possible for the murderer to gain the corridor undetected after Briault had dashed into the boudoir, and before Miss Doty and

Wunsch appeared in the hall. In which case the murderer may have descended the service stairs and escaped from the house; or run up the service stairs in time to mingle inconspicuously with the servants collecting in the upper hall.

But this was presuming that the murderer was either an outsider, or one of the house staff. And Doyle had good reason to feel that his most likely suspects were among the family rather than in the ranks of those who served them.

If he disregarded Wunsch's account of his movements during and after the shooting, it was conceivable that Wunsch might have come from Texie's suite after Briault had rushed into the boudoir, and just in time to be discovered in the corridor by Miss Doty-who took it for granted that the millionaire had emerged from his own quarters.

Or, accepting Wunsch's story but disregarding Briault's, there was time, while Wunsch slipped into his bathrobe and Miss Doty went to see how the old man fared, for Briault to slip from Texie's room into his sister's boudoir. 

Doyle stood up and stretched lustily. Thought was no 174

good unless it resulted in action. Obviously the thing to do was to find the gun that had been hidden in the silver divan. Now. Before there was time to get it out of the house.

He went down to the library and phoned Judge Blain. The Judge was to come as quickly as possible, bringing a search warrant. 

It was not long before Blain's car rattled under the porte-cochère. Dr. Blake had preceded him by a few minutes and was closeted with Paula. Although it was after ten, neither Wunsch nor Briault had put in an appearance. The butler assured Doyle that neither they nor anyone else had been outside the house that morning.

Doyle passed this information on to Blain after he had given the Judge a terse account of the night's happenings. "So the gun is probably still here," he summed up. "Our chances for finding it couldn't be better." "Just what we thought when we looked for the watch," the Judge reminded him gloomily. "Get out your search warrant," Doyle said unperturbed. “We'll notify Wunsch what we're up to, and we'll begin with his rooms."

Adolph Wunsch was breakfasting in bed when they burst in upon him, the butler fluttering and protesting and backing ahead of them to no avail. Nor did 

Wunsch's roars and profanity have any effect. Judge 

Blain produced the warrant which Wunsch perused irately and threw to the floor, with the announcement that he'd get in touch with his attorneys and make it damn uncomfortable for somebody. He went off huffily into his dressing-room, and presently they heard him 175

singing off-key under the shower-whether from bravado or a forgiving disposition, they could not judge. 66 Maybe it's because he knew we wouldn't find anything here," Blain concluded sourly as they departed for Briault's apartment.

Briault was as enraged as his brother-in-law had been. And he insisted on remaining to watch the search, doing his best to ridicule and hinder it. In his bitter, mocking fashion he drew their attention to possible hiding places-snatching his books from the shelves to prove that nothing lurked behind them, tapping the floor and walls to assure them there were no false partitions. "Keep your hands off things," Doyle ordered sternly.

And eventually Briault flung out of his room leaving it to their mercy. They found him later sitting by his sister's side, holding her hand, quietly talking to her in French. 

Doyle asked Paula's permission to search her dressing-room and boudoir, involuntarily apologizing for the disturbance to her. For Paula's appearance shocked him. It was almost incredible that the events of one night could change her into a wan apathetic woman who took no interest in anything and objected to nothing.

The search continued all day. It ranged over the entire house in minutest detail, disrupting order, leaving confusion and indignation in its wake. For the servants were as indignant as the family. Not one of them, man or woman, but regarded the search of his personal possessions as a direct accusation. Of the entire 176

household, only Paula and the elder Wunsch acceded willingly to the ordeal.

Even Miss Doty was difficult, annoyed with Doyle, not for going through her things, she said, but for subjecting the old man to indignity. "I don't know whether you're just pigheaded or plain dumb," she confronted the detective stormily. "Didn't I tell you he was in bed fast asleep when the shot was fired? Even suppose he was bluffing sleep, how could he get from the balcony into his room without me knowing it? " "Calm down, Red," Doyle advised her. " I'm searching every inch of this house and you might as well take it in your stride." 

She protested hotly, " But I am. Did I say a word when you made a shambles of my bureau drawers? Or when you snitched some coffee when you thought I wasn't looking?' 99 Doyle grinned and patted her back consolingly. But she jerked away from his touch. "An old man ready to pop off isn't going around murdering people, and you know it. He shouldn't be dragged into a mess like this. You'll get him all worked up. Look at him now." She pointed into the sittingroom where the elder Wunsch was seated at the window, his Bible open on his lap, his gnarled hands clutching his cane, his chin resting on it, his puzzled eyes following Blain's manœuvres. "Does he know what we're looking for?" Doyle asked the nurse. "Don't you dare to tell him," she warned. "He thinks there's been a robbery. Mr. Wunsch said not to tell him the truth." 177

Doyle nodded. "O.K." 

Miss Doty softened a little at that." You look tired. 

I bet you didn't have any lunch." "Haven't time," Doyle told her, his hands busy poking into upholstery, opening drawers, lifting rugs, shaking draperies.

Miss Doty eased out of the room and returned with sandwiches and milk. "Tasted mighty good," Blain acknowledged as he laid his napkin carefully aside and wiped his lips on his handkerchief. "Tasted like it was drugged," Doyle said teasingly, and then, as Miss Doty whirled on him indignantly, he added by way of atonement, "We might get someplace with this search if we put you to work on it." 

She followed his meaning look toward the old man and her face brightened. "I found his cane this morning," she whispered. " Behind his bed." 

Judge Blain stood up and looked at Doyle disapprovingly. "Can't waste all day talking to ladies," he reminded." Got the first floor still to do." 

Doyle winked at Miss Doty and followed the Judge to the door. "Maybe you'll have better luck downstairs," the nurse said sympathetically. "I'll wish hard for some for you. 99 

But that evening as Doyle dined at the Seaview, he thought morosely that Miss Doty's good wishes were not very potent. He and Blain hadn't found a damn thing. Apparently there wasn't a firearm in the house. 

Unless that locked room behind the stairs was an arsenal. That was the only place they hadn't searchedfor the reason that Wunsch had the only key, and 178

Wunsch had taken occasion to disappear by the time they discovered the locked apartment.

Doyle had wanted to break the door down but Judge Blain had flatly refused to countenance such violence. 

After all, Beachville was largely dependent upon Wunsch's taxes and largesse. There was a point beyond which he should not be pressed, Blain insisted, until there was a clear case against him. 

Doyle gave in willingly enough to this counsel. The fruitless search had tired and discouraged him. He had felt torpid all day and the dull buzzing in his head had continued to bother him. A touch of indigestion, evidently. 

For Dr. Blake's tests of the coffee taken from Miss

Doty's room revealed no drugs present. Of course, the doctor pointed out, only the brew from which the nurse and Doyle had drunk might have been tampered with. 

But Doyle thought this unlikely. Miss Doty had been constantly in and out of her room during the making of the coffee; and Doyle had kept watch on the corridor which ran past her door. It was hardly possible that anyone could have entered without Doyle seeing, or without running into Miss Doty, or awakening the old man asleep in the adjoining 

Doyle's thoughts drew up sharply. The old man could have entered Miss Doty's rooms without going through the hall for Doyle to see, or without arousing undue suspicion on Miss Doty's part.

But the detective shook his head impatiently. It didn't make sense that the feeble octogenarian, blissfully unaware of the attempted murder and full of childish concern for a missing cane, would have any wish or reason to drug Miss Doty. Now if it had been 179

Adolph Wunsch, instead of his father, who had easy access to the nurse's room 

Again Doyle caught his breath as he saw in memory the tableau of Wunsch and his father disappearing into the old man's suite to look for the missing cane. 

Wunsch had lingered only long enough, he had told Doyle, to put his father to bed. But what was to prove that Wunsch had not slipped into Miss Doty's room and doped the coffee so that she would relax her allnight vigil over Paula Wunsch?

More and more Wunsch loomed as the central figure in the mystery. Doyle was increasingly convinced that it was because of Wunsch that Follins was furtive and Briault terrified and Paula distracted. It was Wunsch who had sent his daughter and his mother-in-law away from Beachville. And who had, by one means or another, rid his home of Helen Voss. And it was Wunsch who now disappeared on mysterious nocturnal jaunts. 

Where did he go? Why was he being trailed by two men? 

Doyle groaned. His body ached for sleep and his mind balked at the thought of action. But he dare not slow down now.

When dinner was over, he beckoned Ellen up to their room and explained briefly what he had in prospect. "There's no telling when you'll see me again," he concluded.

Ellen listened quietly. She knew of old that set to his jaw and that tautness in his voice. He was doing what he felt he must do. 180

Helen began to unravel her knitting with hands which shook." There's nothing strange about that,” she protested. "It's just that Texie is having such a wonderful trip." "How do you know what kind of a trip she's having?" Doyle challenged. "Or where she is, for that matter? You don't hear from her, you've just told me." 

Helen swallowed and moistened her lips. " But-but her father hears from her." "And naturally, what Mr. Wunsch knows, you know," Doyle said suavely. 

She recoiled as if he had struck her. "I've told -" she began, and finished desperately, “Please you go." "In a moment," Doyle agreed. He leaned forward and peered into her face. "Tell me. Isn't Texie in this country? On the quiet? 99 Helen came to her feet with the swift thrust of a spring uncoiled. One hand pressed tight against her heart. "NO!" she said emphatically, passionately. “Of course not." But her eyes could not meet Doyle's. "O.K.," the detective said. He picked up his derby. "May I have a glass of water before I go? 99 192

Her hand fell to her side and she nodded with quick relief, then turned and went through one of the doors into a kitchenette. He could hear the water running and he could hear her banging at the ice tray in the refrigerator, trying to dislodge it. Now was his chance to find out if she was alone in the apartment. 

He moved noiselessly to the other door and put out his hand to open it. But with his fingers just closing on the doorknob, he felt it turn under his hand. He stepped back quickly, watching the door.

It opened the merest crack and he saw the narrow margin of a woman's face peer through at him, and the next instant heard her low whisper. "Don't let them fool you. They're keeping me prisoner here. You must find 99 But before she could finish the sentence, the sound of running water stopped. The woman broke off abruptly and drew back. 

The door closed soundlessly. 193

a Doyle stood in a doorway watching the entrance to the Wildwood, mulling over the astonishing climax to his call on Helen Voss. 

Who was the woman behind the door?

Doyle had seen little of her face and this little none too clearly. For she stood with bright lights behind her and the visible portion of her features in shadow. 

Doyle had, however, gleaned four things. The woman was tall, vigorous, middle-aged, and accustomed to giving orders. Her head had been almost on a level with his own. Her hand on the door was strong and steady; but the long, vividly manicured fingers were lined and tight-skinned. Her voice, even in that hurried whisper, was authoritative.

How was it possible for a slight young girl to hold such a woman prisoner? Surely she would have sufficient wit and resource to deal with a jailor as unformidable as Helen Voss.

Yet the caution the woman had used in making her presence known to Doyle had indicated some measure of fear and helplessness-sufficient to put constraint 194

upon Doyle's impulse to drag her and her story out into the light, and to send him away, too puzzled by this sudden development to know how best to employ it. 

When he had approached that closed door, his expectation had been that he might discover Texie Wunsch hiding behind it. But from the moment the door began to open, he had known intuitively that it was not Texie who opened it. 

Perhaps this woman was a relative of Wunsch's. One of those psychopathic cases that rich families keep in seclusion and never mention, sparing themselves embarrassment and annoyance. And what more likely than that Helen Voss was caring for the sick woman? All of which would explain the sumptuously furnished apartment hidden away in a lower-middle-class neighborhood, and Helen Voss' claim that she was doing confidential work for Wunsch.

But what was to prevent the woman, psychopathic or not, from voluntarily ending her imprisonment if she found it so distasteful that she must appeal to Doyle for release? When Doyle arrived, the door to the apartment had stood unlocked and open. If the woman was a prisoner, why didn't she just walk out? Helen Voss didn't have the physical strength to stop her. 

Of course, when Doyle rang the bell in the entry, both women must have thought that it was Wunsch returning, and it was easily understandable that the prisoner would not attempt escape with Wunsch so close at hand.

Then, too, there seemed to be added confirmation of imprisonment in the heavily curtained windows. Or it might be argued that they were closely screened merely to keep Wunsch's presence in the apartment secret. 195

Again it was significant that Helen Voss would not admit Doyle to the living-room until she had banished the woman from it. But why had the woman submitted quietly to banishment? Why didn't she refuse to be shut away? Why didn't she take advantage of Doyle's presence to walk out and openly accuse Helen Voss and 

Wunsch of holding her prisoner?

Could it be that the story they might tell in explanation would involve and condemn the woman more than . it would them?

Perhaps this woman had to place herself at their disposal because they knew something about her from which they were protecting her. Suppose, for instance, this woman had killed the young girl whose bones Doyle had found in Wunsch's woodland? And Wunsch, for family reasons or otherwise, was protecting her-hiding her from the police? And Helen Voss was conniving in the matter because of her indebtedness to Wunsch? 

Doyle scowled and shifted his weight from one tired foot to the other. The trouble with all this slick supposing was that if this woman were actually being hidden from justice because she had killed the girl in the woods, then who had tried to kill Paula Wunsch?

Had the woman escaped last night-made her murderous attempt—and returned to the Amsterdam Avenue apartment? But surely in that case she would not make her presence known to Doyle. 

What had motivated her appearance at the door? 

What had her interrupted warning been intended to convey? "You must find" she had said, and then shut herself away leaving the sentence unfinished. How would it have ended? 196

Find what? 

Or find whom? 

Doyle lit a cigarette and puffed at it moodily. Maybe if he'd try to piece the story from what the woman did say, instead of pondering what she didn't say, he'd get somewhere.

Her exact words were, "I'm being held prisoner here. You must find " And it seemed to him that there had been an inference in her words that if he found such-and-such or so-and-so, she would be released. 

Of course the very first words she said were, "Don't let them fool you." "Them " obviously meant Wunsch and Miss Voss.

The woman was warning Doyle that they were lying to him. True, Wunsch hadn't been there to lie, so the inference was that for Wunsch's sake or acting on his instructions, Helen Voss was lying. 

Doyle quickly reviewed what Helen had told him. 

For one thing, that she wasn't Wunsch's mistress. 

And this Doyle was inclined to believe. Certainly the other woman's presence in the apartment took the lovenest aspect out of the whole setup.

Helen Voss had also said, with alarm and emphasis, that Doyle was wrong when he suggested that Texie was in this country. 

Was that it, Doyle thought? 

Don't let them fool you. In other words, Texie is in this country. That's why I'm being held prisoner here. 

To release me you must find Texie. 

Doyle took off his derby and mopped his brow. He felt out of breath-as though he had run a long distance. 

Well, anyway.

If this woman wasn't a nut and knew 197

what she was talking about-who the devil was she?

And why was she being held in an Amsterdam Avenue apartment because Doyle hadn't found Texie, when presumably Texie was in Paris with Mrs. Briault? 

With Mrs. Briault.

Doyle dwelt on that name, transfixed by it. Then he took to fanning himself vigorously with his derby. 

It was Mrs. Briault behind that door. 

Sure it was. The picture he'd seen of her had shown her in profile, so it wasn't much wonder he hadn't recognized the little he'd seen of her face. But her tallness was like Truman's. And the something arrogant underlying her speech and manner. And the Briault dependence upon Wunsch for money, that would make her do his bidding against her own wishes. sure, Doyle told himself excitedly, it all checked. Sure,

He felt so good over this discovery that there was temptation to gloat over it. But it was imperative for him to force his tired mind onward from this point. At long last he was beginning to get somewhere. 

Wunsch was hiding Mrs. Briault unknown to everyone but Helen Voss. Why? Because Wunsch had some reason to fear Mrs. Briault? Feared what she knew? 

Feared what she could tell the police? Because Mrs. 

Briault knew that Texie Wunsch had killed a young girl in her father's woods, more than a year ago?

Was that what Paula Wunsch had realized when she heard Doyle speak of the wrist watch? Was it to shield her husband's daughter that she refused to tell Doyle who the dead girl was? And had Texie found out that her guilt was known to Paula? Was that why she had tried to kill her stepmother? "Damn it," Doyle said forcibly. "No. Not Texie.” 198

He'd studied the young girl's face. Even in a smudgy newspaper print, her quality came through. Hottempered, she'd be. She might conceivably kill. But it would be in open battle. She wouldn't fire when her victim's back was turned, or from ambush hidden by the night.

The trouble was, Doyle told himself irritably, he was looking in the back of the book for all the answers before he had so much as solved the first problem. 

Who was the skeleton?

He could make lots of pieces of the puzzle fit into lots of fancy patterns—but he never found a place for that particular piece, whether he dealt with facts, or whether, as now, he let himself rave and rove in fantasy. 

Doyle was saved further brain racking by the advent of the Rolls coming smoothly to a stop before the canopied entrance of the Wildwood. As Wunsch's portly form passed through the doorway, a wild burst of music and hilarity bowled forth into the quiet of the street. 

Doyle's over-tired nerves twinged and winced. How did Wunsch do it, night after night?

But for all his immobility Doyle was wide awake and thinking furiously.

Who was the woman Wunsch was seeking in the night clubs? Why must the search be so secret that he dared not hand it over to the Missing Persons Bureau or a private agency? What was its nature that he must keep it secret from his wife? Didn't it occur to him that she would grow suspicious of his frequent absences and that she would have him followed? Al was trailing him? Was that why there had been an attempt to kill Mrs. Wunsch?

Doyle's thoughts went off on a tangent. He recalled 

Wunsch's first hostile reaction to the discovery of the skeleton, and his subsequent anxiety to believe that one of his own servants had been murdered, and his relapse into sullen fury when the housekeeper disproved that theory. What was the key that would reveal the mystery of this strange moodiness?

Suddenly Doyle experienced a rising excitement-the struggle and tantalization of a borning conclusion. Like a child repeating a reading lesson, he found himself fashioning words under his breath. "Wunsch is seeking a young woman. A young woman was murdered on Wunsch's property." 

And then, with a surge of relief and certainty, Doyle told himself, "The murdered woman is the woman Wunsch is seeking." 

He doubled back then, carefully checking his hypothesis by the known facts.

Wunsch did not find the woman he sought as he made the rounds of the cafés. Because she was dead. 

Although Wunsch did not know this, or perhaps consciously suspect it, it was the reason why the discovery of the murdered girl had terrified him and made him so difficult to deal with. For it brought to birth the first intuitive feeling that this might be the fate of the girl he sought.

That explained his eagerness to believe that a servant had been murdered. And his despairing surliness once that possibility was removed. It also made understandable and pitiful the dogged determination which drove him, joyless and dejected, from one café to an- 202

other, obsessed with the desire to find the one he sought, to assure himself of her continued existence. 

Who was she? 

Who was her murderer?

Was it from the same source that death had struck at Paula Wunsch?

Doyle frowned and sighed and fought off the dead weight of weariness. But as night lifted and the dawn came up, it was only to the first of these questions that he could give an answer. 203

Doyle changed the subject abruptly. "When did you bring your mother-in-law back here?" "Six months ago." "She told me she's being held prisoner in that apartment." 

Wunsch grunted indignantly. "Nonsense. She's free to come and go. Within that neighborhood. Or elsewhere, for that matter, if she can finance herself." His eyes met Doyle's candidly. "You can see how it is. I made it clear to my mother-in-law why I couldn't have her seeing her family or friends. The only way to protect Texie was to pretend that they were still together travelling abroad." "Then wouldn't it have been wiser to leave your mother-in-law in Europe? Why did you bring her here?"

Wunsch tapped the fingers of one hand nervously against the other. "I got to thinking that Texie was asking for so much money because she was splitting with someone to keep them from betraying her whereabouts to me. So I watched Truman-checked his mail -saw to it that he didn't come into town alone. That yielded nothing. Then I decided to test my mother-inlaw. I figured if I ordered her to come back here to stay in hiding, she would refuse if she could finance herself abroad with Texie's help. But my mother-inlaw came and she stayed. And it's damn evident she wouldn't put up with Amsterdam Avenue if she was getting any money from Texie." A wry smile twisted 

Wunsch's lips. "Heaven knows what Texie is doing with the money." 216

Doyle spoke impetuously, "For God's sake, Mr.

"I'm doing you a poor kindness," Doyle declared, "not telling you that the bones of the skeleton were those of a girl between the ages of 18 and 25. Five feet six. Perfect teeth. And the murder was committed on or about the 13th of June of last year-the day your daughter was to sail." 66 Wunsch's head was sunken on his chest and his eyes stared blindly. Once he said, “ Texie?" in a questioning quivering tone. And then, "Murdered?" His face twisted. He put one hand over his eyes.

Doyle stared out the car window. He wished that this ride were over and done with. 

Wunsch did not speak again until they slackened speed entering the village of Beachville. Then he raised his head and looked about him dully. "Where do you want to go?" he asked. "The Seaview."

Wunsch pulled the speaking tube toward him and directed Follins. Then he just sat there absently pulling the tube back and forth. 

Doyle cleared his throat. "I guess you know I'm damn sorry for your trouble, Mr. Wunsch," he said. 

Wunsch nodded. Then all at once his head turned sharply and his grief-blurred face fused with sudden purpose. "Help me," he appealed to Doyle. " Find the one who killed her." 217

a 

Doyle slept all day oblivious to the Sunday bustle of refined boarders going and coming between the beach and the hotel. He arose in time for dinner. And after dinner he telephoned from Dr. Blake's house to 

Adolph Wunsch.

Wunsch's voice came over the wire, weary and hopeless. "I haven't told anybody anything," he said guardedly. "I've been waiting to hear from you.' 99 "That's right," Doyle approved. And arranged to have Wunsch send a car in the morning to transport his mother-in-law and Helen Voss to Beachville. 

As Doyle turned from the telephone, Dr. Blake moved forward, his pince-nez gleaming excitedly. The doctor wanted to suggest that the murder of Texie Wunsch was a sex crime. The doctor had spent his summers at Beachville for years. He had seen Texie grow from a hoydenish child into a flamboyantly attractive young woman-warm-hearted and sparkling with gayety, but impetuous and indiscreet. Badly brought up, the doctor said, shaking his head. Poor 218

girl. What could you expect? Deprived of her mother in childhood and alternately over-indulged and overrestricted by her father. Her schooling had ended abruptly. She'd been expelled for reasons unknown but not unguessable. For Texie wasn't above flirtation with anything in pants. Even her father's servants. 66 I've seen her parked in a car on a back road with that young chauffeur of Wunsch's," the doctor gossiped. "And I suppose you've heard she had Mrs. 

Wunsch's brother on her string? And God knows who else." "I'll get those details from Helen Voss tomorrow," 

Doyle planned. “But I'm not sure they're the important angle. Because they exclude a key point—the attack on Mrs. Wunsch. It's not impossible that Texie's love life could involve her stepmother. But there's another likely motive for murderous plots against a millionaire's daughter and his wife." "Money?" Dr. Blake queried. 

Doyle nodded. "Unless," the doctor suggested dubiously, " the attack on Mrs. Wunsch was a bluff. It seems queer to me that when the murderer missed killing her, he didn't fire at least a second shot."

That was much what Truman Briault had suggested, Doyle recalled, as he went back to the Seaview and early to bed, his brain still too tired to deal with the more complicated possibilities of the mystery. 

But the next morning he arose full of vigor and was just finishing a hearty breakfast when Follins drove the Rolls up the Seaview driveway.

Follins explained that Mr. Wunsch had ordered him to call for Doyle before driving into the City to move 219

the ladies from the Amsterdam Avenue apartment. So 

Doyle waved good-bye to Ellen and was driven off as the long line of rockers on the Seaview porch came to a standstill, momentarily paralyzed by the grandeur of his departure.

Wunsch was pacing the terrace impatiently waiting for the detective. The two men went directly to the locked room behind the stairs-the room which Doyle and Blain had been unable to enter. As Wunsch turned the key in the lock and the door swung inward, Doyle surveyed the apartment with astonishment. In sharp contrast to the rich furnishings of the house, this room was sparsely supplied with shabby office equipment. 

Wunsch took a cloth from a drawer of the old-fashioned roll-top desk and dusted an oak chair for the detective. "I don't let the servants or anyone in here," he explained. "These things are from my office in the old brewery. I keep track of my own affairs." He gestured toward a row of filing cases shoved under shelves of dusty ledgers." My father was a poor man. He taught me the value of money and how to hold on to it.” 

Doyle nodded, thinking that in the light of his recently acquired knowledge, there would be little reason to search this room for the murder weapon.

Quickly then he reviewed with Wunsch the details of Texie's disappearance, and went over the reports turned in by his predecessor on the case. Only someone in close contact with you could know and trade on the fact that you had no suspicion of your daughter's fate. It was someone who lives here who knew when the skeleton was discovered, and when I began my investigations. The proof of this is that you have received no more letters over your daughter's signature. The murderer did not dare risk writing again." 

Wunsch raised his head. His face was livid. "But in that case," he said slowly, "we're none of us safe now." 221

Doyle took the paper and pen from her and went out into the corridor, closing the boudoir door behind him. Standing at the hall window he compared Paula's writing with Texie's. They were not unlike. Big, bold, and square, both of them. Doyle stowed the papers his pocket and stared abstractedly over Wunsch's rolling acres to the blue intermingling of Bay and horizon. in 226

He had gone to interview Paula Wunsch with black suspicion of her in his heart, and with sudden credence for Dr. Blake's suggestion that her attempted murder was a trick. For it was fantastic to suppose that an eighty-year old man would scheme and murder for his son's fortune. And it seemed obvious that it was 

Paula who would most benefit by Texie's death. 

But Paula's strange moods, her ambiguous admissions, her erratic denials, had made a puzzling impression upon Doyle. She didn't act like a woman who was full of plans to get her hands on a fortune. The effect she gave was of a woman betrayed and disillusioned. 

Betrayed by whom? Disillusioned by whom? 

Doyle rubbed his chin reflectively. Had Paula and her mother and her brother conspired together to get 

Wunsch's fortune? 

Doyle knew that the Briaults had planned to marry 

Texie to Truman. And that Wunsch had balked their plan. Was the subsequent murder of Texie merely a horrible impulse which sprang from bitter disappointment over the failure of the plan? And were the fake communications from Texie to her father merely desperate subterfuges to conceal the unpremeditated murder? Or had the Briaults murdered for the sake of the money Wunsch had been hoaxed into paying? Did the 

Briaults expect to play that game long enough to make it yield richly? 

No, Doyle told himself, they couldn't be that dumb.

The drainage of cash from Wunsch was a side line. 

And Texie's murder was neither meaningless nor impulsive. If the Briaults were involved in it, then certainly its motive was to get at Wunsch's fortune. 

The only flaw in this plausible theory was Paula's 227

divorce plan. Paula could divorce Wunsch and claim alimony whether Texie were dead or alive. And whether 

Texie were dead or alive, the Briaults stood to lose money by Paula's divorce. For there was nothing to prevent Wunsch remarrying and leaving the bulk of his fortune to his third wife.

So it would seem that Paula, Truman, and Maman were not acting in concert. 

Perhaps in the beginning they had been, when they planned to marry Texie to Truman. But the failure of that plan may have split the conspirators. Certainly that day on the golf course Paula and Truman were at variance. Paula had argued for bringing Truman and Texie together in Paris. But Truman had rejected the idea violently. Was not that proof that Paula knew nothing of the murder of Texie until she heard Doyle speak of Texie's watch? And was it not equally reasonable to suppose that Truman had opposed Paula's suggested trip to Paris because Truman knew that 

Texie was dead?

Then it might be that only Truman and his mother were conspirators. When the marriage to Texie fell through, had they plotted to kill her, knowing that Texie's marriage to any other man would divert the 

Wunsch fortune away from the Briaults? Had Maman deliberately let Texie escape from the Philippe, and had Truman lured Texie into the woods and shot her, to make certain that Paula would be Wunsch's only heir? 

Doyle snapped his fingers excitedly. That would make Paula's reactions clear and understandable. Paula, being innocent of Texie's murder, had risen passionately and honestly to her own defense when Doyle tried to im- 228

plicate her. And she had likewise spoken truly when she claimed to have had no suspicion of the reason for her husband's nightly absences. Also, her attempts to protect her husband from knowledge of his daughter's fate had been as sincere and disinterested as they had been foolish.

But what was the explanation of the other things Paula had said and done? What explained Paula's collapse when she learned the dead girl was Texie? Paula's attempt to bribe Doyle not to solve the mystery of the murder? Paula's recent distaste for her brother's company? Paula's sudden retraction of her admission that she had received letters which led her to believe that 

Texie was alive?

Those letters had come from Maman, Doyle surmised. Written by Maman according to Wunsch's instructions, and mailed from abroad at his connivance. 

But Paula did not know this. She would destroy the letters now at the first opportunity. Paula would consider the letters evidence against her mother. For it was clear that Paula thought her mother and brother guilty of the murder of Texie.

That was why Doyle's threats could not move Paula nor draw response from her. She would never bear witness against her family, and for herself she had neither fear nor hope. She believed herself to be doomed. 

Because the murderous attack on Paula had not been a ruse. And Paula's apathy and despair were the reflection of Paula's terrible belief that her brother feared betrayal at her hands. And that her brother would, if necessary, send her where he had sent Texie.

Before Doyle could re-examine this theory, he heard his name quietly spoken and wheeled to find the butler 229

standing behind him. Mr. Wunsch was waiting downstairs. He would like Mr. Doyle to join him at luncheon.

Doyle followed the butler's stately descent. Adolph 

Wunsch stood in the lower hall, the bright sunlight beating mercilessly on his upturned face, revealing its expression so haggard and so fearful that Doyle was momentarily startled. "What's the trouble?" he queried sharply. 

Wunsch answered him tonelessly. "Nothing." He led the way into the dining hall. 

At their entrance, Truman Briault, seeing Doyle, rose from his place at the table and left the room. 

It was not until Wunsch and Doyle were seated that Doyle recalled the words Wunsch had spoken in the shabby office, and knew how to interpret the look on 

Wunsch's face. "We're none of us safe now," the millionaire had said. 

And with that remembrance Doyle realized that his reconstruction of a possible plot to get Wunsch's money had stopped short of the terrifying conclusion that now the Briaults would find their path to Wunsch's fortune straight and unobstructed if Wunsch himself were to die. 230

The dank, moldy contents of the bag proved to be a box of face powder, a jar of cold cream, a comb and toothbrush, lace-trimmed pajamas, a jewel case and a purse. The jewel case was empty. He slipped it into his pocket. Then he stowed the rest of the things back in the bag and picked it up. "O.K.," he said to Follins. "I'll search Briault's room for the jewelry."

Then he began to question her seriously and in detail: about the noises that had brought her to her door late at night; about Mrs. Wunsch's earlier hostility; about Truman's gallant attentions; about the old man's knowledge of his granddaughter's liaison. 

Miss Doty's answers were brief and straightforward, but not particularly informative. Her fiercely protective feeling for the old man was continually evidenced in the undertone of her voice, in the undercurrent of her thought-causing Doyle's suspicion to merge into 257

certainty that the old man had indeed informed on Texie, and that Miss Doty knew as much but would not admit it for fear of implicating him in the murder. 

As for Doyle's other questions, Miss Doty made it clear that she held no resentment against Mrs. Wunsch for her earlier suspicions; no complaint against Truman nor interest in him; and no explanation of the mysterious night noises. "You're a big help," Doyle told her, disgusted, as he moved along the corridor to Briault's room. He eyed her darkly. " From now on," he announced, "I'm giving you as little information as you give me."

He shut the door in her crestfallen face, and surveyed the room with little hope, remembering how thoroughly he and Blain had already searched it. But all at once it came to him that during the first part of that search Truman had been present, impeding their progress, sarcastically commenting on their methods, and making clownish pretense of helping them. 

For the first time it occurred to Doyle that perhaps that pretense of help, that surface clowning, had been deadly earnest effort to keep them from finding something. 

The detective pivoted on his heel, his gaze sweeping the four walls. Just where had Briault concentrated his foolery?

Doyle recalled the young man thumping at the walls and tapping the floor with his knuckles as he announced that there were no secret passages. And there was 258

something else he had done about which Doyle had spoken to him sharply. What the devil had that been?

Doyle's quick movement toward the bookcases was interrupted by a discreet knock at the door, and a servant entered carrying a luncheon tray. He pushed a spring, and legs sprouted from the tray, converting it into a table, which he placed in the window alcove. "What's all this?" Doyle inquired. 66 

Mrs. Wunsch's orders, sir," the man said.

When the footman had withdrawn, Doyle dropped to his knees before the neat rows of books. He remembered that it had been the lower shelves which Briault had interfered with. Now he saw that the bottom row held a handsomely bound edition of many volumes, lettered in gold, ARABIAN NIGHTS—BURTON. Doyle grimaced. No wonder Briault had grabbed these. He wouldn't want he-men to see that he read fairy tales!

Twenty-four volumes of them, too. The sap. With quick contemptuous movements, Doyle removed the set and found the space behind it clear. It wasn't until he began to shove the books back that he noted one of them was considerably heavier than the others. Hefting it on one palm he flipped at its pages, then muttered an excla- 259

mation, scrambled to his feet, and carried the book over to the windows. There was dried glue roughening the gold-edged pages, binding them together. 

Doyle lifted the cover. Except for the first few pages he found that the text had been cut from each sheet and the margins glued together. In the box-like hollow thus contrived lay the jewels that had belonged to Texie's mother; and among them a platinum wrist watch minus wrist band and clasp.

So Follins' indictment of Briault was true, Doyle mused, turning now with ravening appetite to his luncheon. Briault had sought to gain financial ease by marriage to Texie. But when that fell through and his bitter disappointment had driven him into a rash quarrel with her, he had pulled himself together and made new, desperate, hasty plans. He had effected a reconciliation with Texie and persuaded her to meet him at the brewery, bringing with her everything she possessed of value. The scheme had been as bungling as you might expect a scheme of Briault's to be. It hadn't occurred to him, of course, that Follins might loiter and spy and show up at the scene of the murder. He hadn't figured on using Texie's money to buy protection. Nor had he realized that he would not dare dispose of her jewelry. He had not even had the wit to realize that he dare not keep it. So here it was, mute conclusive evidence against him, evidence which would send him to the Chair. 

And thinking this, Doyle heard a sound at the door. 

Briault, at last, he thought. Let him come. Doyle was more than ready for him-set to drag confession from him. 260

But it was not Briault's soft step across the floor. It was Miss Doty's.

Doyle stood for a moment frozen with unbelief and horror. Then his mind grasped the enormity of what he had seen, and he flung headlong out to the terrace and across the lawn. He knelt beside Briault and raised his head gently. Blood was flowing from a chest wound. 

Doyle tried to stanch the wound with his handkerchief.

But he knew there wasn't much he or anyone could do. 

In fact, he ought not to be here, he told himself grimly, regretting the natural impulse which had sent him to the stricken man. The shot had been fired from the house. Doyle ought to be in there now, hunting down the one who fired it.

He was aware of a murmurous uproar gathering force within doors. There was a sound of running feet, of agitated voices. And as though the confusion penetrated to his consciousness, Truman Briault stirred and groaned. Doyle saw his eyelids flicker and the bloodless lips move. "Take it easy," the detective said. 

The poor devil was almost gone. But the stubborn instinct for life remained. He was trying to say something. It was cruelty to let him speak, to do anything to recall him from the merciful oblivion awaiting him. 

But Doyle had a duty to perform. He put his lips close to the dying man's ear. "Tell me," he said. "Just one word. Who did this?" 

Truman's weight sagged heavily on Doyle's arm.

Careful inspection showed that each of these sections was securely closed. 

Doyle's attention shifted to the heavy folds of stiff velvet which flanked the window. Anyone standing behind them would be concealed from view in the hall, but clearly visible to someone coming across the lawn to the house. Had Truman looked up and seen Follins pointing a gun at him? 266

LE a 

Doyle had the happy faculty which most men and few women possess he could escape from trouble into sleep. And, usually, when he awoke he found himself able to deal with whatever had been disturbing him in of anger or worry or failure. the way 

But the morning following that fateful Tuesday yielded no panacea. He opened his eyes with the dawn and found his mind still dark and confused by the mysterious deaths of Texie Wunsch and Truman Briault. 

It seemed logical that the falsities in Follins' account of Texie's death, plus his disappearance after the killing of Truman, plus Truman's dying statement, added up to proof of Follins' guilt. But the chauffeur's alibi, vouched for by the family of the murdered couple, made that summation preposterous. The last people on earth to furnish Follins with a fake alibi would be 

Paula and Adolph Wunsch.

So Follins was innocent unless, Doyle mused, an accomplice of Follins had fired at Truman. Doyle had accused Follins of telling the story of Texie's murder 279

in reverse of switching his rôle for Briault's. This theory still appealed to Doyle; and now he considered wistfully the possibility that Follins had commissioned someone to kill Briault, because Follins feared that

Doyle would learn from Briault that it was Follins who had killed Texie. But it didn't make sense, the detective was forced to admit, that Follins would take on a new accomplice to rid himself of an old; he'd be no better off than before. 

Forget about Follins, Doyle admonished himself, and look at the facts. Texie and Truman, dead. And only by chance was Paula Wunsch not with them. So the plan, as disclosed thus far, was to kill Wunsch's wife and daughter and brother-in-law. Why these three?

Could it be that they had combined, knowingly or unknowingly, to injure a fourth person? Obviously Truman had been aware of an enemy when he told Doyle he dare not talk because his life was endangered. But 

Texie had gone fearlessly to meet her death. And Paula had blurted, as she kneeled beside her brother's body, that her fear had been of him.

Was this fear of Paula's founded upon partially astute suspicion? Had Truman conspired with another to get at Wunsch's fortune, and refused to go ahead with the conspiracy when he found it involved murder? 

With whom would Truman conspire? 

With Follins? But how could Follins hope to benefit by the deaths of Texie and Paula? The chauffeur's relationship to Wunsch was only the pleasant ordinary one of well-paid servant to well-served master. Moreover, Wunsch had said explicitly that if Paula were to die now, there would be only his father left to inherit. 

Doyle's jaw thrust forward grimly. Now Wunsch's 280

father had been stricken and lay on the brink of death. 

Was that pure coincidence? Or had the old man's stroke been induced by threat or attack? Had the old man collapsed in terror when a gun was pointed at him? And had the murderer then stayed his hand, content to wait for the inevitable passing of the last obstacle to the goal of the Wunsch millions? 

Who might conceive a mad plan to remove Wunsch's heirs, and then, by winning Wunsch's favor, inherit his fortune legally? 

Doyle let his thoughts rove at random and brought up short at the image of the arch Comtesse de Briault. 

Could the empty aging creature have such insane illusions concerning her artificial charms as to imagine she could win Wunsch's regard and his fortune for herself? 

Would her avarice and vanity carry her to such dreadful lengths? Doyle rejected the wild notion distastefully. That was too horrible, too unnatural, to believe even of the Comtesse. 

What's more, such lusty scheming and ruthless killing would indicate the vigor and hope of a younger woman, sure of her charms, young enough to play a waiting game, and already so well ensconced in Wunsch's favor that the outcome could be anticipated and the terrific risks run for the almost certainty of great gains. 

Considering these specifications Doyle was again confronted in his thoughts by the image of a womana young woman-a personable woman-for whom Wunsch had given evidence of trust and affection. But again Doyle violently rejected unwelcome fancy. He'd had years of experience judging human nature, and Helen Voss, he told himself fiercely, was O.K. 281

So his mind lurched away from the thought of her and went blank and unhelpful for some moments. Until it recalled an item he had completely forgotten. Texie's notebook. 

A bit excitedly, he leaned out of bed to the chair on which his coat was flung, and pulled it over to get at the notebook which he had tucked in an inner pocket. 

His movement disturbed Ellen, who flounced over, pulling at the sheet. Doyle bunched it over her, propped his pillow against the head of the bed, and opened the book. He turned the pages slowly, reading everything, somewhat let down by the innocuous character of the inscriptions. Shopping lists. Fittings. Hairdressings. 

Notations of luncheons and matinées. 

Doyle noted that Helen Voss accompanied Texie on all of these excursions. Apparently Texie's unconventional conduct had cut her off from the companionship of other girls. Doyle observed, also, that the dates of these engagements covered the first eight months of the previous year to within a week of the sailing of the Philippe.

Flipping the last blank pages hurriedly, Doyle found that a folded paper had been wedged between the outer leather binding and the inner leather lining of the back cover. With fingers which fumbled with excitement he poked the paper out and unfolded it. 

What he read sent him bounding from the bed, utterly oblivious to Ellen's indignant protests as the sheet trailed with him. For he held in his hand, properly executed and duly stamped and sealed, a certificate of the marriage of Texas Wunsch and Henry Follins. 

It was dated the day of the Philippe's sailing.

282

that the discussion would sink into the Wunsches' thoughts and stir up recollection which would prove helpful. • • He had a brief and none too friendly session with the District Attorney, who was resentful because he had not been notified immediately upon discovery of the skeleton, and who was openly suspicious of Doyle's dogged insistence that all he could contribute in the way of help was the canvas, the wooden heels, and the three bullets.

Together they went to Judge Blain's to get his report on the bullets. As usual, the Judge was a busy man. He seated his visitors on nail kegs in the barn, while he tended his concrete mixer and grumbled because he must take time off that afternoon for the inquest.

Doyle only half listened to the Judge's description of certain involved technical tests he had made with the bullets and a sheet of lead foil, which proved that the bullets had all three been fired from the same weapon.

The hammer and bore of the weapon had left incontrovertibly identical markings on each bullet. "But I might say," the Judge pursed his lips cautiously, "that in my opinion there's something queer about the gun that fired those bullets." 

At that Doyle came to alert attention. He and the 

District Attorney demanded simultaneously, "What's queer about it?" 

Blain said darkly, "Doesn't it seem queer that only one shot was ever fired at a time? 99 

Doyle replied impatiently, " Two times out of three, only one shot was needed. In Mrs. Wunsch's case, the murderer didn't dare wait to fire twice." 297

The Judge shook his head in disagreement. “ I'd say the murderer didn't fire twice because he didn't have a second shot in his gun. And I'd say it was nothing but darn good marksmanship that picked Texie and Truman off the first crack. But the law of averages made him miss Mrs. Wunsch. And don't tell me he wouldn't have fired again if he'd been able. "For instance, take a fellow goes out after a deer. 

And he sights one and aims and fires. Maybe the first shot brings the deer down but, like as not, you'll find he's fired twice. Part reflex action of his trigger finger, and part just making certain he's got his quarry. "Seems to me the same would be true if he's after a man. If he'd run the risk of firing once, he'd run the risk of firing twice. Don't take only a second to aim and fire again.” 

The District Attorney said peevishly, “Why would a murderer handicap himself with a one-barrel pistol when it's easy enough to get a revolver?" "Who said anything about a pistol?" Blain inquired. "My opinion is these bullets came from a rifle. 

Forty-four calibre. Foreign make." "Foreign?" Doyle pounced. "The bullets or the rifle?" “Can't tell about the rifle 'til I see it," Blain said. "But the bullets weren't made over here, that's sure."

Doyle rose and paced the laboratory's narrow confines. "Serves me right," he muttered. "Right under my nose and I paid no attention to him."

Dr. Blake peered through his gleaming pince-nez. "You don't think the old man knows anything about the murders?" he asked incredulously. 

Doyle nodded grimly. "I think he knows all the answers." 

That afternoon was taken up by the inquest, which recorded that Texas Wunsch and Truman Briault had 299

died at the hand or hands of a person or persons unknown.

The double murders were as manna to the gentlemen of the Press; but the District Attorney's brief elation had given way to disgruntlement. 

Wunsch had sworn there were no firearms on the estate, nor anyone interested in them. The deer with which his park was stocked were just for atmosphere, he maintained,—not for sport. The District Attorney let it be known that in his opinion Wunsch was uneasy under questioning and was concealing something. And the District Attorney's men were searching the house and outbuildings and grounds for weapons or bullets or both.

The next day, Friday, the search was temporarily suspended in deference to the funeral services. 

Doyle attended, at Wunsch's request, and noted the District Attorney's close observation of the mourners who gathered about the two coffins. 

The Comtesse de Briault, as might be expected, put on a good show. She entered the oppressively flowerscented drawing-room, a queen of tragedy in dramatic black, knelt for an ostentatiously long period beside the white casket which held Texie's bones, praying earnestly, Doyle surmised, for the forgiveness of the girl's multifarious sins. But when the Comtesse arose to kneel beside her son's body, a suitable and terrible grief overcame her and she had to be led swooning to a chair, where she sat leaning heavily on her daughter throughout the remainder of the service.

Paula's face was carved of stone and her eyes were dry with feverish brightness, as if they had been drained of tears and were burning out in agony. 300

Wunsch's broad shoulders shook, and he wept openly throughout the simple service.

Helen Voss maintained an admirable composure even when they carried the bodies through the gardens and over the brow of the hill, to lay them to rest in a sheltered cove. But when Texie's coffin was lowered under its blanket of white orchids, Helen suddenly broke and her slender body was racked with sobs.

Miss Doty moved over and put her arm around the girl's shoulder. Miss Doty did not cry, though her face showed great strain. And directly the service was over, she hurried ahead of the others back to the old man who soon would be laid beside these two. 

Doyle returned to the Seaview. He could not find it in his heart to do anything that day. In truth, he had a strong inclination never to return to the Wunsch estate. Why not drop out quietly with some trumped-up excuse? He knew that the District Attorney resented his presence on the scene and would welcome his withdrawal.

So why not dump the whole business on the District Attorney?

By the time he blundered through to the distressing half-conclusions Doyle had reached, the old man would have been laid to rest beside the murdered lovers, and the mystery of their deaths might be effectually buried with him. 301

But the next morning Doyle returned to the Wunsch estate to carry on his self-elected task, let the guilt fall where it might so long as it fell justly. 

He went into conference with Adolph Wunsch in the locked room behind the stairs. Wunsch was, as the detective had expected he would be, puzzled and put out at Doyle's insistence on an interview with his father. "What can you hope to learn from him?" Wunsch questioned with some asperity. "Even if he could speak, what could he tell you? We've kept everything from him. He believes Texie is still travelling abroad. When he was awakened by the attack on Mrs. Wunsch I told him we'd had a robbery. He doesn't know anything at all about Truman's death." 

Doyle nodded wearily. "I know. But I must talk to him-must try to establish some communication with him," he maintained doggedly. " Dr. Blake tells me the hearing of the left ear has not been affected. So I ought to get some reactions to what I say. You'll have to leave it to me to know how to interpret those reactions." 302

"He's so old," Wunsch protested. "So close to death. And except for the few years of comfort I've been able to give him, his life has not been an easy one. 

I don't want it to end on a note of horror." He looked at Doyle pleadingly; but Doyle would not let himself be swayed. Justice was found, most of the time, down roads men would consider it easier not to travel. 

So the outcome was that Dr. Blake and Judge Blain were summoned; and accompanied by them and

Miss Doty looked sharply into their grim faces as she rose from her chair at the head of the bed. "What is it?" she demanded, moving between them and the still form as if to prevent their nearer approach to the dying man. "Out of the way, sister," Doyle told her.

She took note of the set of his jaw and her momentary defiance dwindled. "I'll be in my room," she said quietly," if you want me." "Better stay here," the detective ordered, and Dr. 

Blake nodded agreement.

The old man lay like a log. His body, outlined under the bed covers, seemed to have stretched, to be almost inhumanly attenuated. The awful grimace of paralysis contorted his face. The only movement was that of his left eye-a rolling, questioning orb, somehow terrifying to gaze into. 

Doyle seated himself in Miss Doty's chair, Wunsch stood behind him, and Dr. Blake and Blain moved to the other side of the bed. 

Doyle began by saying in slow, calm speech that the old man must not try to talk unless there was something he very much wanted to tell them, when he heard 303

the questions they were going to ask. Then the detective added, "If you can hear and understand me, try to give some sign."

Excitement and gratification stirred in the watchers as the old man lifted his left hand in a barely perceptible movement and let it fall back on the coverlet. 

Doyle prefaced his first question by saying that there had been trouble here. Trouble which concerned Texie. 

Could the old man help them find Texie? Did he think she had met with an accident? Back in the woods, perhaps?

But though they watched him closely, hopefully, the dying man gave no further sign or response. Even that one seeking eye had ceased its movement and was staring glassily into Doyle's.

The detective introduced a new topic. Did the old man know that Mrs. Wunsch had an enemy? Did Mrs. 

Wunsch deserve to be punished for something? Was 

Mrs. Wunsch dangerous? Whom would it benefit if 

Mrs. Wunsch were dead?

It was discouraging to pound away at these questions, struggling to state and restate them provocatively, only to have them achieve no result. 

Perhaps, the doctor suggested, the movement of the old man's hand had been inadvertent, not intended as a response. It was quite possible that nothing Doyle said was getting over to him. 

But, doggedly, Doyle played his trump card. He introduced Truman's name. And he asked without preamble if the old man knew that somebody had killed Truman?

Unmistakably, this question had been heard and comprehended. None of the watchers could say just 304

what it was that happened. For, at first, that one weird roving eye did not change its wild fixed gaze, nor the feeble hand make a gesture. Nevertheless, they all knew instantaneously that the old man had a definite reaction to that question.

And then his feeble hand began to beat at the coverlet and his eye rolled wildly. The room itself seemed to quiver with the terrible inner struggle he was making to break into speech.

Doyle felt his forehead bedewed as if the effort were racking his own body. He looked across at Dr. Blake. 

Both the doctor and Blain showed strain. And Miss

Doty, leaning against the door, looked as if she would faint. 

Suddenly Wunsch took the initiative. "Let me," he said.

He began to question his father in German, repeating often the name of Truman. The old man's excitement increased until it seemed the terrific pressure of his desire for communication must release the power of speech. Wunsch murmured on encouragingly in guttural, unintelligible talk. 

And suddenly he turned to Doyle. "Get paper," the millionaire said sharply. “My father will try to write with his left hand.” "Paper and a soft pencil," Doyle snapped at Miss

Doty. But she merely looked blankly at him, apparently too dazed, too worn by the scene to comprehend and act. Doyle pushed past her into the sitting-room. He went to the desk and quickly snatched up writing materials.

They arranged paper and pencils on a bed tray spanning the old man's knees, while his revolving eye 305

sought their faces as though he were drawing help and strength and confidence from them.

But again and again, as he tried to manipulate the pencil, it fell from his feeble grasp. 

Wunsch explained with tears in his eyes, trouble is, he's naturally right-handed.” "The They were all affected by the scene. It was somehow heart-rending to see the old man's desperate determination handicapped by his appalling weakness. "Perhaps you could guide his hand," Dr. Blake suggested. "As you would a child's."

The suggestion proved an excellent one. Wunsch covered the old man's mottled fist with his, and after some meaningless scrawls, reported in hushed tones, "We've got the hang of it. He's written the letter 'D." "
