Three more letters took form. The fourth came reluctantly. And the fifth was too much. The old man's nerveless fingers could no longer clasp the pencil. His hand collapsed on the paper, hiding what he had written.

Wunsch slipped the sheet of paper gently from beneath the spread, powerless fingers, and the others crowded around him, frowning down at the undecipherable scrawl. "What the hell? " Doyle muttered, his tone weighted with disappointment. 

Miss Doty giggled irrepressibly. "That's not writing," she commented. "That's nothing but a lot of scribbles." "It's German script," Wunsch explained huffily. "I can read it.”

He moved over to the window. "S-T-O -" he spelled, and paused to decipher what followed. “—C - -" he called. And then with a downward inflection, concluded triumphantly, "-K.” "Stock?" Doyle repeated blankly. "What's that mean?" 

Wunsch threw the paper aside dejectedly. "All that agony for nothing," he said. 

Doyle repeated, "What does stock' mean?" "Der Stock," Wunsch corrected mechanically. "Cane. Walking stick." 

Miss Doty said, "Oh!" in a gasp; and Judge Blain let his breath out in a gust of disappointment. The 307

tension in the room slackened noticeably. Miss Doty moved away from the bed. Wunsch and the doctor and 

Blain sat down as if they were tired. 

Only Doyle and the old man seemed to have any continued interest in the experiment. They were looking at each other, deeply, steadily. 

Adolph Wunsch said sadly, "That cane has always been an obsession with him. One of the few things he brought from the Fatherland. He'd never let it out of his sight. A childish whim. Old people are like that.” "Whim be damned!" Doyle spoke harshly. "He knows what he's doing. He's telling us to find that cane." The detective turned to Miss Doty. "Where is it?" he snapped. 

Her eyes flashed angrily at his tone. "How do I know?"

Nevertheless, she came over to the bed and bent to peer beneath it. Then she straightened. "Sometimes it falls under there," she said more mildly. "But I don't see it now." "When did you see it last?" Doyle prodded.

She looked vague. "I don't think I've seen it since he had the stroke," she answered. "But I don't really know. It may have been around here after that. I've had my hands full without worrying about his old cane." 

Doyle nodded. "O.K. We'll find it." "But do you think -?" Miss Doty looked from her patient to Dr. Blake. “We can't do him any harm," Dr. Blake reassured her.

And indeed, as they began to search the room the old man seemed to react favorably to the commotion, 308

his one eye following their movements with lively interest. It was Judge Blain who found the cane, after they had twice gone over the three rooms and two baths which comprised the old man's suite and Miss Doty's quarters. "Only one place left we haven't looked," he remarked.

The others stared at him incredulously, and Wunsch said irritably, "I'd like to know where the hell that is?” "Your father's bed," Blain told him. "But you can't move him," Miss Doty protested; and Dr. Blake shook his head dubiously. 66 

Doyle looked appealingly at the doctor. " Figure it out some way," he begged.

Dr. Blake decreed that the best they could do would be to shift the old man from one side of the bed to the other without removing him from it. In that way he would suffer no more discomfort than during a change of bed linen. Miss Doty insisted that she be allowed to effect the shift, which she did dexterously and with a protective affection for her charge which was both womanly and touching.

But as it turned out, the old man need not have been disturbed at all; for the cane was not concealed in the bedding, but under the bed on the heavy wooden slats which braced the mahogany frame. It lay across the slats where they rested on the ledge of the long side piece, and it was not visible in the gloom beneath the heavy piece of furniture. It was only by running his hand along the ledge that Blain found the cane. 

He handed it to Doyle without comment, and the detective hurried over to the windows. The others waited 309

in silence, watching him turn the old man's stick over and over in his hands.

Presently he beckoned to Blain. "Ever see one like this?" he inquired. 

Blain examined the walking stick briefly. "I've read about these," he said. "They're not made in this country. Illegal."

Wunsch and Dr. Blake came to peer curiously over the Judge's shoulder. "Good Lord!" Dr. Blake ejaculated in surprise. "It's a rifle."

At that Wunsch turned yellowish-white and moved over to stand by his father. 

Blain sighted along the shaft of the cane. "Shooting sticks, they call these," he remarked. His finger felt and found the trigger ingeniously concealed in the crook of the handle. "Single barrel," he reported. 

Doyle went over to Miss Doty. "What do you know about this?"

She was leaning weakly against the window casing. 

Her face was deathly pale, her body trembling. "Look," she said shakily. "Can't we keep it quiet? 

Can't you see it the way I do? He's going to die anyway. " Her eyes met Doyle's pleadingly. "That's why 

I didn't tell."

Over by the bed Wunsch laid his big hand protectively over the old man's. "I saw him that day," Miss Doty confessed. "When he killed Truman. That's what brought the stroke on.

He knew I had seen him and he went to turn on me, and the excitement was too much for him." 

Doyle said admiringly, "Well, I'll be damned."

She seemed oblivious of his amused scrutiny as she 310

hurried on, her voice low and tremulous. "Can't we hush it up? For Mr. Wunsch's sake?"

Dr. Blake interjected, "There's something in what she says, Doyle. The old man won't live the week out." 

Doyle chuckled. "There's more in what she doesn't say."

Still grinning, the detective put one hand heavily on the young woman's shoulder and with his other hand tilted her face up to him. "Why did you do it?" he demanded.

For a moment she eyed him with the look he knew so well of mingled astonishment and indignation. But under his knowing, mocking gaze her pose crumpled. "Why did you do it?" Doyle demanded again. 

She stood at bay, her lips clamped, eyes smoldering. "O.K., sister." Doyle pushed her down into a chair and stood looming above her. "If you won't tell me, I'll tell you. It was you and Truman Briault. Before 

Texie took him away from you." 99 Instantly, goaded and infuriated, she rose to his baiting. 66 

Texie couldn't take him away from me," she denied. "I had only to look at him, and he'd be begging and coaxing me to come to his room, even though he'd been with her that afternoon." 

She tossed her vivid head arrogantly. " All he wanted of Texie was her money. And it was his sister kept pushing him into that." The nurse turned her contorted countenance upon Wunsch. "That's all your wife thinks of. How to get her claws on your money. 99 "You got your claws on about ten thousand of it," 

Doyle accused suavely. "What did you do with that dough, sweetheart?" 311

Her fury subsided as quickly as it had arisen and she looked at the detective with a wary, secretive expression. "What are you talking about?" "Listen, Innocence." He spoke in level tones which held an undercurrent of menace." It will be a damn sight more comfortable for you if you tell me of your own free will where that money is." "Don't you threaten me," she warned angrily. "You haven't got a thing on me that you can prove." She jerked her head toward the bed. " He won't be here to testify against me. And I'll sit in Court with my legs crossed high and swear he did it." She looked boldly into Wunsch's face." Unless you'd like to call the whole thing off right now, pay me what it's worth, and sign a paper that I'm clear of all charges." 

Wunsch could only blink in astonishment at her effrontery. But it was evident that her threats impressed him.

Doyle spoke to the millionaire sharply. "Don't pay any attention to her bluff. Get your housekeeper.”

While they waited for the woman to come, Blain crawled under the old man's bed and ran his hand exploringly around the bed frame. He emerged dustily, with a pleased gleam in his eyes and a small cardboard box in his hand. "I figured these must be somewhere," he remarked as he uncovered the box and rolled the few bullets it contained on the palm of his hand. "Good work," Doyle applauded. He addressed Miss 

Doty quizzically, "Saving those for somebody, Sweetness? 99 She glared back at him. "I wish somebody would put them through you, Pet." 312

"Darned if I ever knew," Blain said with unaccustomed loquacity," that a woman could be such a crack shot." He looked at the red-headed girl with unwilling admiration. "Where under the sun did you learn to handle a gun?" 

She tossed her head with a pleased smile. “The old man taught me. The first summer I came. I wasn't here two weeks before I found out about the cane and why he didn't want it out of his sight. He was afraid he'd get into trouble because he smuggled the cane in when he came to this country. He carried it in his hand right under the Customs Officer's nose. And the bullets in his pocket. Just because he wanted something to remind him of his old life. So when I promised not to tell on him, I got him to teach me how to aim and fire. Just to pass the time away. That was before Mr. Wunsch remarried, and the old man and me had the place pretty much to ourselves." She looked at Doyle defiantly. "I didn't know there was such a person as 

Truman Briault then," she said. "So you won't be able to make anything out of that in Court." She smiled maliciously and with growing confidence in what she clearly regarded as her impregnable position. 

The housekeeper entered. Doyle turned to her eagerly. "Do just as I tell you," he directed. "Take Miss

Doty behind that screen and strip her. And as fast as you get her things off, throw them out to me."

The housekeeper suppressed a gasp and looked toward Wunsch, who nodded uneasy confirmation of the order. 

Miss Doty gripped the arms of her chair and twisted her feet over its rungs. But Doyle yanked her to a 313

standing position and held her at arm's length as she struggled and kicked and clawed at him. "You might as well take it easy," he admonished, and when she redoubled her efforts he shook her lightly. 

At that she threw back her head and began to scream. 

66 Shut up, ," he told her. "I owe you worse than that for drugging my coffee." "Prove it," she shrieked. "Prove I drugged your coffee. You damn bully. You can't prove anything against me. I'll sue you for this. I'll sue the bunch of you. You call yourselves men and you stand by and see a woman treated like this." 

Doyle said with deadly quiet," Keep this up, and I'll search you myself. So help me, I mean it."

She quieted at that, gave him a baleful look and disappeared around the screen, followed by the housekeeper, whose professional aplomb even this ordeal could not shake. "Throw it out as you get it off," Doyle called. 

A moment later the starched uniform and apron fell at his feet. Then the flat-heeled shoes and sheer hose. 

He was examining these articles methodically when the housekeeper called to him. "Mr. Doyle! Come quick!"

Doyle bounded behind the screen. Miss Doty, in shorts and brassiere, was doubled over, her arms crossed against her face. "She put something into her mouth," the housekeeper said.

Doyle forced the nurse's head up and held her nostrils until her jaw dropped. He put his finger into her mouth and fished out a partially chewed pulp of pink cardboard. 314

"O.K.," he said to the housekeeper. "This is what I was after. Let her get dressed."

He went back to the others and smoothed out the wet cardboard. The impress of the girl's teeth had not obliterated the text which identified it as a check issued by a checking room in Grand Central Station. "What's so important about that?" Wunsch inquired. "It ought to be worth ten thousand to you," Doyle told him drily. 

Miss Doty kicked over the screen. "You think you're so damned smart," she shrieked at Doyle. 

The detective grinned. "Get it now?" he asked

Wunsch." She has the money you mailed to those Post 

Office addresses stowed away in this checked luggage. 

Took care of that the other day when Follins drove her in." 

Miss Doty slapped Doyle's face stingingly. He held her arms pinioned to her sides, shuffling nimbly out of the way of her kicking feet. When she was limp and exhausted, he lowered her into a chair and shook his head sadly over her. "Can't take it, can you?" he mourned. "You're the kind of dame that just wants to hand it out. Better pull yourself together, sweetheart, before I call the District 

Attorney in, to tell him you shot Texie to keep her from marrying Truman. And then you worked out a neat little scheme to finance yourself at Mr. Wunsch's expense, so Truman would marry you. You tried to kill Mrs. Wunsch because you thought she suspected your affair with Truman. When she asked you to send for me, you thought she wanted to report that. After you failed to kill her, you made an impressive demon- 315

stration of nursing her; and it was then that you learned from her that she suspected her brother of the murder and of the attempt on her own life. So you decided you were sitting pretty. Until you saw me in 

Truman's room with Texie's jewelry. I don't suppose you knew the jewelry was in his possession. I don't suppose you knew any of the hell in which your crime had involved him. But the moment you saw the jewelry you knew that I would arrest Truman, and that I'd draw from him the story of his affair with you and recognize your motive for killing Texie. That's why you disposed of Truman. And speaking of Truman," 

Doyle said musingly, "what put Texie wise to him? 

What happened the night she went to his room? "

He had asked the question more of himself than of 

Miss Doty-thinking aloud—having no reason to expect any help from her. But to his surprise she answered with a sudden violent burst of laughter. "I looked over his shoulder straight into her eyes," the nurse said confusingly. "She came to his window and saw me there, lying on the bed in his arms. And he never even knew she'd been there." 

Doyle said distastefully, "Laugh away, sister. It's a good joke. And it's on you. It never occurred to you, did it, that when Truman told you about the elopement plans, he was being tricked by Texie? Because Texie was going to marry someone else and cast Truman back on you. You put yourself to a lot of trouble for nothing." 

For a long moment Miss Doty stared at the detective, the laughter still bubbling from her throat, coming shriller and louder until it merged into a dreadful screaming. 316

Back at the Seaview, Doyle stretched and yawned lustily. "From now on," he told Ellen, "I'm taking things easy. 99 "What?" Ellen said sharply. 66 "After all," Doyle mused, " except for the refining atmosphere and so on, a vacation's no damn good unless a man gets some rest out of it." "It's nice you finally got that through your thick head," Ellen approved. "We go home tomorrow, you know."

THE END
